03. ML Depth and Science Application: Research and Project Deep Dives, and Applying Science to Amazon Problems

Format notes

  • Official frame. Amazon's Applied Scientist interview-prep page describes one or two 60-minute technical phone screens with a senior leader, then a loop of four 55-minute interviews with members of the science community. Each interview covers technical competencies and Leadership Principles (LPs), and the behavioral portion focuses heavily on your past work [1].
  • What "Science Depth" means in practice. Candidates consistently describe the depth round as a deep dive into one or two projects or papers from the resume, usually led by a Senior Applied Scientist.
    • An AS II (L5) Advertising Trust candidate described "Science Depth" as a resume deep dive plus GenAI fundamentals (Transformers) plus the business impact of past projects [3].
    • Other AS2 reports describe a "deep dive into the specifics of my resume and past projects" [22], "modeling details in my project" [11], and the questions "why went with a particular choice of architecture?" [12].
    • IGotAnOffer summarizes the round as walking through a research project, with follow-ups on technical decisions, the modeling trade-offs considered, and outcomes [2].
    • A 2026 aggregate of about 20 candidate reports (2026 Q1 to Q3) describes the depth round as a full hour on your most complex project or your publications. The interviewer reads the resume in advance and chooses the topics: problem framing, data cleaning, baselines, class imbalance, why that backbone, metrics, deployment, and what you would do with more compute. Interns had each publication taken in turn [41].
    • Some loops replace or supplement this round with a 30-40 minute research talk or job talk followed by Q&A [10][16][26]; senior loops more often include presentations [39]. Blind commenters say the recruiter or coordinator should send a brief naming the topics and LPs for each round, so ask for it [19]. The same 2026 aggregate warns that the recruiter's preview of the rounds is often inaccurate [41].
  • Phone screens. 2026 reports describe screens of 30-60 minutes that combine a two-project resume dive (the data, the training problems and why the design was chosen), a theory block and a coding problem [41]. Machine Learning Engineer loops report a separate "project deep dive" round of 10-15 minutes per project followed by layered questions [42].
  • What "Science Application" means. This is the second half of the chapter, and it is reported in two flavors:
    • (a) An open-ended Amazon business or product problem. You propose data sources, labeling, modeling, metrics and risks [2][5][6]. Examples include modeling view, click and purchase probabilities [7]; categorizing 10k products into 100 categories [9]; training retrieval from click logs [9]; trustworthy ad recommendation [3]; and Alexa ASR for accented speech [6]. 2026 prompts include agentic AI proposals, a multi-turn search agent, a chatbot architecture and an evaluation framework for brand names in generated images [41].
    • (b) Discussion "based on past project experience," per a Blind comment [18].
    • An L5 candidate (Nov 2024) listed "ML application" as a distinct onsite round alongside depth, breadth, DSA and Bar Raiser [4]. An Alexa guide lists "science application" as one of five loop rounds [5].
  • Interviewer type. Usually Senior or Principal Applied Scientists, and sometimes the hiring manager (Applied Science Manager), who may run a "tech talk" style mix of design and behavioral questions [3][8].
    • LPs are probed in every round. Older reports say about 5-15 minutes of each interview goes to one or two LP questions [5][27]. 2026 reports describe much more: often about half the hour, so the technical part may not start until minute 25 or later [41][43].
    • The depth round is often paired with Dive Deep, Deliver Results or Earn Trust [41]; Ownership and Customer Obsession also fit it naturally [Likely].
  • L5-specific bar. At L5 (Applied Scientist II), the depth interviewer is checking several things:
    • whether you were "the architect or just a passenger" [23][25], with your decisions separated from your collaborators' and your advisor's [41];
    • whether you can defend every choice (architecture, loss, metric, data split) against alternatives [23][24][41];
    • whether you understand leakage and "how models cheat" [23][24];
    • whether you can connect the science to measurable customer or business impact [3][41][42]. Level is judged here: one candidate passed every round on content but was told the projects lacked the impact expected for the target level. The 2026 aggregate's advice is to lead with the project that has the largest measured business effect [41][42].
    • A Blind commenter notes that a fresh PhD is typically placed at L5 [21]. That means the bar includes publication-level rigor plus evidence that you can ship [Likely].
    • Interviewers often want "plausible and scientific sounding ideas on the why," even when they do not know the answer themselves [20].
  • Recent trend (2025-2026). Depth rounds increasingly drift from your project into the fundamentals behind it. Reported topics include:
    • transformer internals, KV caching, long-context attention (Longformer) and bi-encoder vs. cross-encoder retrieval [12];
    • BERT vs. GPT and DPO vs. RLHF [13];
    • beam search decoding [17];
    • knowledge distillation and learning with limited labels [8];
    • how Segment Anything works [12];
    • for generative-AI and post-training teams, LoRA, RLHF, GRPO, KV caching and distributed training, while ads and seller teams lean on statistics, experimentation and ranking [41].
    • AGI-team loops are reported to lean heavily on modern LLM training and to weight ML depth above behavioral questions [28].
  • Method note (2026-10-14 review). The original pass came from about 38 web searches, using search-result extracts rather than full page reads.
    • In this review, the egress proxy refused every cited host except raw GitHub content (HTTP 403 on Glassdoor, Blind, LeetCode, Medium, Quora, amazon.jobs, amazon.science, IGotAnOffer, Interview Kickstart, Dataford, OA VO Service and others), and the web-search budget was already used up. Those references could not be re-checked. "Unreachable" does not mean dead, so they were kept.
    • Five GitHub-hosted sources were fetched and read in full: [28], [41], [42], [43] and [44]. [28] supports the claims it is cited for. [41]-[44] are new and back the added 2026 questions.
    • Retagging: [Reported] items that cite only a prep-vendor guide ([24], [27]) or a non-science Amazon loop ([38]) were retagged [Likely], in line with the policy below. Two generic "answer a question about our product" items and one near-duplicate were removed.
    • Several sources are prep-vendor guides (Interview Kickstart, Dataford, OA VO Service). When a question appears only in a vendor guide, it is tagged [Likely] with "see [n]". [41]-[44] are TrueInterview pages mirrored on GitHub. They aggregate candidate reports and name the round, but not individual candidates.

Tag legend: [Reported] means a candidate or source reports it for an Amazon Applied Scientist or closely related Amazon science loop (cited). Items reported at Amazon with no role stated are marked "(role not stated)", and items from Research Scientist or MLE loops are marked as such. [Likely] means a well-grounded inference from the role, level, team or Amazon products.

A. Opening the deep dive: framing, scope and ownership

  1. Walk me through the project or paper on your resume that you are most proud of, end to end. [Reported] [2][3][22][41]
    • Describe your recent work and projects. Which would you choose for a 45-minute deep dive, and why that one? [Reported] [2]
    • What was the problem statement, and who was the customer? What was the business motivation? [Reported] [41][42]
    • Which parts did you personally design, implement and evaluate, and which were done by others? [Reported] [41][42]
    • If I asked your collaborator or manager, how would they describe your contribution?
  2. Pick two projects from your resume. For each one, what was the data, what training problems did you hit, and why was the design chosen? [Reported] (phone screen, 2026) [41]
  3. Describe one of your proudest projects. What's the most innovative thing you did in it? [Reported] [2]
    • What made it novel compared with the prior art or the existing baseline?
    • Was the novelty necessary, or would a simpler baseline have got most of the gain?
  4. Tell me about a project where you were the single-threaded owner of the science. [Likely]
    • What decisions did you make without being told to?
    • What would have happened to the project without you?
  5. What was the state of the art or the production baseline when you started, and how far did you move it? [Likely] (see [26])
    • How did you choose the baselines, and were they tuned as carefully as your method? [Reported] [41]
  6. What was the hardest technical obstacle in this project, and how did you get past it? [Likely]
    • What did you try that failed?
    • How long did you spend on dead ends before pivoting? (Bias for Action and Dive Deep)

B. Problem formulation and data

  1. How did you turn the business or research question into an ML problem (classification, ranking, regression, generation, RL)? [Likely] (see [41])
    • Could it have been framed differently, and why did you reject the alternative framings?
  2. How large was the training data, and where did it come from? How did you clean it? [Reported] [41] (see also the Glassdoor depth-round reports summarized at [12])
    • How did you sample it, and is the sample representative of production traffic?
    • How did you down-sample or construct the dataset? [Reported] [7]
  3. How did you obtain labels, and how noisy were they? [Likely] (see [2])
    • Did you measure inter-annotator agreement?
    • How did label noise affect your model, and did you correct for it?
  4. How would you build this with very little labeled data? [Reported] [8]
    • Would you use weak supervision, semi-supervised learning, active learning, or LLM-generated labels?
    • How would you validate synthetic or LLM-generated labels before training on them?
  5. How did you handle class imbalance in your project? [Reported] [27][41][42]
    • Why did you choose re-weighting, re-sampling or threshold tuning over the alternatives?
    • How did the imbalance change your choice of evaluation metric? [Reported] [27][41]
  6. Which features mattered most, and how do you know? [Likely] (see [24])
    • Would you trust impurity-based or permutation importance here, and why? [Reported] (phone-screen theory block) [41]
    • How did you guard against features that are not available at inference time?
  7. How did you handle missing data, outliers and distribution differences between data sources? [Likely]
  8. Would you standardize your inputs? How did you treat sparse numeric features, and what did you do about correlated features? [Reported] (role not stated) [44]

C. Modeling choices and alternatives

  1. Why did you choose this particular architecture or backbone? [Reported] [12][41][42]
    • Why X over Y? What concrete evidence made you choose? [Likely] (see [23])
    • What would a simpler model (logistic regression or GBDT) have achieved?
  2. What alternatives did you consider, and why did you rule them out? [Reported] [41] (see also the Glassdoor depth-round reports summarized at [12])
    • Did you run the alternatives, or reason about them?
  3. Why did you use one gradient-boosting library or framework over another? [Likely] (see [23])
  4. If you used a pretrained model, why that backbone, and how did you adapt it (full fine-tuning, adapters, LoRA, prompting)? [Likely] (see [28][41])
    • How did you choose the size of the model given latency and cost limits?
  5. Derive or write out the core equations of your method on the whiteboard. [Likely] (see [25][41])
    • Which assumptions does the derivation rely on, and which of them does your data violate?
  6. What are the main hyperparameters in your model, and how sensitive are the results to each? [Likely]
    • How did you tune them, and on which split?
  7. If you used knowledge distillation, what exactly was distilled and why? [Reported] [8]
    • What were the teacher-student gap, the temperature and the loss mix?
    • Why distill instead of quantize or prune?

D. Loss, optimization and training dynamics

  1. Why did you choose that specific loss function? [Likely] (see [23])
    • How does it align with the metric you actually care about?
    • What happens if you swap it for a ranking loss, focal loss or a calibrated probabilistic loss?
  2. Tell me about loss functions and optimizers you used, and why. [Reported] (see the Glassdoor depth-round reports summarized at [12])
    • Why Adam or AdamW over SGD with momentum? [Reported] (phone-screen theory block) [41]
    • What learning-rate schedule did you use, and why?
  3. Did you see overfitting or training instability, and how did you diagnose and fix it? [Likely]
    • Training accuracy is high but test accuracy is low: what do you do? [Reported] [2]
    • Your model is underperforming: diagnose and fix it. [Reported] (listed as a depth-round question) [41][42]
  4. How was training distributed and how long did it take? What was the compute budget? [Likely]
    • Explain the parallelism strategies and collective operations your training used. [Reported] (role not stated) [44]
    • What would you do with more compute or resources? [Reported] [41][42]
    • What would you change if you had 10x less compute?

E. Evaluation and scientific rigor

  1. How did you evaluate the model, and why did you pick those metrics? [Reported] [41][42] (see also the Glassdoor depth-round reports at [12] and [25])
    • Choose between precision, recall, F1 and ROC-AUC for this problem, and justify your choice. [Reported] [27][41]
    • How does the offline metric relate to the online business metric? [Reported] [41][42]
    • Can you write code that computes AUC from scores and labels? [Reported] [14]
  2. How did you confirm the model wasn't just memorizing training data? [Likely] (see [23])
  3. How did you handle data leakage in that specific pipeline? [Likely] (see [23][42])
    • How did you split train, validation and test (random, temporal, by user, by product)?
    • Give an example of leakage you found or almost missed.
  4. Are your improvements statistically significant? [Likely]
    • How many seeds or runs did you use, and what is the variance?
    • What confidence interval or test did you use?
  5. What ablations did you run, and which component contributed most? [Likely] (see [26])
  6. Where does your model fail? Show me the error analysis. [Likely]
    • Which slices (new users, long-tail products, low-resource locales) are worst?
    • What would you do next to fix the worst slice?
  7. Was the model A/B tested online? How did you design the test? [Reported] [41][42]
    • What was the treatment, the primary metric and the guardrails, and how long did the test run?
    • Your online A/B result fell short of the offline gain. How do you find out why? [Reported] [41][42]

F. Deployment, scale and post-launch

  1. How was the model deployed, and what were the latency, throughput and cost constraints? [Likely] (see [24][41])
    • What did you trade away to meet latency?
  2. How did you monitor the model after launch? [Likely] (see [23])
    • How would you detect data drift vs. concept drift? [Reported] [21]
    • What triggers retraining, and how do you roll back?
  3. What happened when the data distribution shifted (seasonality, Prime Day, new marketplace)? [Likely]
  4. If you had to scale this 100x (more traffic, more locales, more products), what breaks first? [Likely]

G. Impact, business value and reflection

  1. What was the business impact of this project, in numbers? [Reported] [3][41][42]
    • How did you attribute the impact to your model rather than to other launches?
    • Who used the output, and how did it change their decisions?
  2. What would you do differently if you started this project again today? [Likely] (see [25][42])
    • Which limitations did you not mention in the paper or launch doc? [Likely] (see [41])
    • What was a real mistake in this project, how was it caught, and what changed afterward? [Likely] (see [42])
  3. Tell me about a time your results disagreed with a stakeholder's intuition. How did you resolve it? [Likely] (see [1][27])
  4. How did you decide this project was worth doing over other options? [Likely]
  5. Tell me about a time you simplified a scientific solution to ship faster. What did you give up? [Likely] (Invent and Simplify)

H. Research talk, job talk or paper round (when the loop includes one)

  1. Present your PhD or research work in about 30 minutes, then take ML-focused Q&A. [Reported] [10]
    • For each publication, how did you choose the baselines, and are they the strongest available? [Reported] [41]
    • For each publication, what was your specific contribution vs. your co-authors'? [Reported] [41]
  2. Present a project of your choice (a "tech talk"). [Reported] [16][12][28]
    • How would this research transfer to an Amazon product?
  3. Why did you make this specific choice in your presentation? (Reported as an interviewer picking one detail and asking "why" repeatedly.) [Reported] [20]
    • What would you expect to happen if this assumption were removed?
    • Can you propose a plausible explanation for this unexpected result?
  4. Paper-read round: discuss a paper's method, assumptions and weaknesses. [Reported] (listed among depth-round questions for FAR and "premium" loops; the exact prompt was not captured) [41]
  5. What is the next paper you would write on this line of work? [Likely]
  6. Which reviewer criticism of your paper was most valid, and how did you address it? [Likely]

I. Depth in your specialty: NLP and LLMs (common for 2025-2026 L5 loops)

  1. Explain the transformer architecture in detail. [Reported] [15]
    • Explain the attention mechanism at "root level," including scaled dot-product, multi-head attention and positional encoding. [Reported] [13][15]
    • Walk me through the mathematical formulation of self-attention. [Likely] (see [23])
  2. How do BERT and GPT differ (objectives, architecture, use cases)? [Reported] [13]
    • How is BERT trained? [Reported] [9]
    • What are the differences between BERT and RoBERTa? [Reported] (see the Glassdoor depth-round reports summarized at [12])
  3. How does decoder-only inference work, and what is the KV cache? [Reported] [12]
    • Why does standard self-attention get expensive on long inputs? [Reported] [12]
    • How does Longformer, or sparse or local attention, handle long context? [Reported] [12]
  4. Compare bi-encoders and cross-encoders for retrieval and re-ranking. [Reported] [12]
    • When would you distill a cross-encoder into a bi-encoder? [Likely] (see [34])
  5. How does beam search decoding work for LLMs? [Reported] [17]
    • What are the trade-offs vs. sampling (temperature, top-k, top-p)?
  6. What is the difference between DPO and RLHF? [Reported] [13]
    • Walk through an SFT training pipeline and its loss. [Likely] (see [27])
    • What does DPO's beta control? [Likely]
  7. If you did post-training: why does GRPO remove the critic, what does that cost, and how would you design the reward? [Reported] (phone screen, generative-AI team) [41]
    • Which instabilities did you hit in GRPO-style training, and how did you fix them? [Likely] (see [41])
  8. Explain the main parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques, which parameters each trains, and when each beats full fine-tuning. [Reported] (online assessment, resume-based) [41][42]
    • How do you choose the LoRA rank and which layers to adapt? [Likely] (see [25][28])
  9. How does an inference engine such as vLLM raise serving throughput for large language models? [Reported] (online assessment, MLE loop) [42]
  10. How do you evaluate an LLM-based system (hallucination, faithfulness, helpfulness)? [Likely] (see [28][32])
    • When would you trust an LLM-as-judge, and how would you calibrate it?
  11. Explain an AI-agent project you built, and how it was adopted. [Reported] (MLE project deep dive) [42]
  12. Why do LSTMs beat vanilla RNNs, and why did transformers replace both? [Reported] (see the Glassdoor reports summarized at [12])

J. Depth in your specialty: search, ranking and recommendations

  1. You have a large collection of user click data for many search queries and product documents. Is click data alone sufficient? How would you train a retrieval or IR model with the data at hand? [Reported] [9]
    • How do you correct for position bias in clicks? [Reported] [42] (see also [29][40])
    • What other biases (query intent, presentation, trust) contaminate click labels? [Likely] (see [29])
  2. Walk through how you would design, train, validate and deploy a ranking model for product search. [Reported] [40][42]
    • How would you estimate true relevance from clickstream logs with position bias? [Reported] [40]
  3. Walk me through a search-ranking project you led: the pain point, why this model, the offline and online metrics, and how you diagnosed a disappointing A/B result. [Reported] [41][42]
  4. Why did you choose pointwise, pairwise or listwise learning-to-rank in your project? [Likely]
    • How do NDCG and MRR relate to online engagement?
  5. How do you handle cold start for new users or products in your recommender? [Likely]
  6. How does matrix factorization for recommendation work, and when would you use it over a two-tower model? [Likely] (see [25])
  7. Predict the click probability for music recommendations. [Reported] (role not stated; phone screen, Sep 2026) [44]

K. Depth in your specialty: vision and multimodal

  1. Describe how SAM (Segment Anything Model) works. [Reported] [12]
    • How would you adapt it to a product-image task with few labels?
  2. You have 10k product listings across 100 categories, each with a title, description and photo. How would you categorize them? [Reported] [9]
    • How do you fuse text and image signals?
    • How do you handle a long tail of categories with very few examples?
    • How do you evaluate when the category taxonomy itself is noisy?
  3. For a detection system you built, explain the backbone choice, how you handled class imbalance, and what more resources would change. [Reported] [42]
  4. Compare approaches to building joint image-text embeddings (CLIP-style contrastive pretraining and others). Where would they help Amazon? [Reported] (online assessment) [41]

L. Depth in your specialty: forecasting, optimization and causal inference

  1. Explain your forecasting model, and how you produce and evaluate uncertainty (quantiles, calibration). [Likely] (see [30][31])
    • Why probabilistic forecasts rather than point forecasts for inventory decisions?
  2. What is the newsvendor problem, and what is its solution? [Likely] (see [38]; reported in Amazon supply-chain interviews, not a science loop)
    • How does a quantile forecast connect to the newsvendor critical ratio?
  3. How would you model an optimization problem from your work: objective, decision variables and constraints? [Likely] (see [27])
    • When would you use a heuristic instead of an exact solver?
  4. Build a package-allocation model for couriers. [Reported] (role not stated) [44]
  5. Prove that a new allocation method outperforms the manual baseline. [Reported] (role not stated) [44]
  6. Design an end-to-end regression model for energy demand. [Reported] (role not stated) [44]
  7. Design a causal study to measure the impact of reminders on customer behavior. [Reported] (role not stated) [44]
  8. How would you estimate the causal effect of a price or promotion change without a clean experiment? [Likely] (see [31])
    • When would you use double ML, instrumental variables, or difference-in-differences?
    • How do you validate a causal estimate you can never observe directly?
  9. In your project, how did you separate correlation from causation in the impact claim? [Likely]

M. Science application: reported Amazon problems

  1. A user moves from viewing to clicking to purchasing a product. Model the probability of each action given a user and a product. [Reported] [7]
    • Would you build three independent models, or model view, click and purchase jointly? [Reported] [7]
    • How would you construct the dataset, choose features, and down-sample (for example with reservoir sampling)? [Reported] [7]
    • How would you handle the selection bias that purchases are only observed after clicks?
  2. Design a system that recommends trustworthy ads to Amazon users while maintaining brand safety and compliance. [Reported] [3]
    • Walk through the full data pipeline, from collection to deployment. [Reported] [3]
    • How do you trade off ad revenue against trust and safety errors?
  3. How would you improve Alexa speech recognition for non-native speakers and users with strong regional accents? [Reported] [6]
    • Where would you get diverse accent training data, and how would you handle phonetic variation? [Reported] [6]
    • How would you measure improvement per accent group without hurting the majority?
  4. You are given a high-level ML problem. Propose a solution, including data sources, annotation strategy, modeling techniques, evaluation metrics, and trade-offs and risks. [Reported] [2]
  5. Propose agentic AI solutions for a hypothetical project, and defend their viability. [Reported] (2026) [41]
  6. Design a multi-turn search agent, and explain how it handles long conversation context. [Reported] (2026) [41]
  7. Architect an AI chatbot: intent detection, dialogue management, generation and a knowledge base. [Reported] [41][42]
    • How do fallback behavior, evaluation and continuous learning work? [Reported] [41][42]
  8. Design an evaluation framework that checks whether a text-to-image system spells a requested brand name correctly and places it naturally. [Reported] [41][42]
    • How would you combine OCR accuracy, embedding-based semantic checks and a human review protocol? [Reported] [41]
  9. Design an LLM quality validation system. [Reported] [41][44]
  10. Design a distributed training-data pipeline (reported for a FAR loop). [Reported] [42][44]
  11. How would you evaluate adding video ads? [Reported] (role not stated, Nov 2025) [44]
  12. How would you measure product-market fit for Alexa Shopping? [Reported] (role not stated) [44]
  13. Design an end-to-end spam detection system. [Reported] (role not stated) [44]
  14. Discuss LLM decoding and training choices in the context of an Amazon use case (LLM team). [Reported] [17][28]
  15. Design and evaluate an ML pipeline to improve personalized product recommendations on the Amazon homepage. [Likely] (see [25])
    • What is the north-star metric, and what are the guardrails?
    • How would you trade off customer engagement against sales? [Likely] (see [25])

N. Science application: likely Amazon problems by org (L5, 2025-2026)

  1. Rufus (shopping assistant): how would you build retrieval-augmented answers to "What do I need for cold weather golf?" over the catalog and reviews? [Likely] (see [32][33])
    • How do you ground answers in product facts and avoid hallucinated specs?
    • How do you evaluate it offline before an A/B test?
  2. How would you summarize thousands of customer reviews into a short "customers say" blurb? [Likely]
    • How do you measure faithfulness and coverage of minority opinions?
  3. Fraud and abuse: how would you detect fake reviews or abusive sellers with heavily imbalanced and adversarial labels? [Likely]
    • How do you handle adversaries adapting to your model?
  4. Supply chain (SCOT): how would you forecast demand for millions of products, including new ones with no history? [Likely] (see [30][31][37])
    • How would forecast errors propagate into buying and placement decisions?
  5. Pricing: how would you design an experiment to measure the effect of a pricing algorithm when prices interact across products? [Likely] (see [31])
    • What about interference between treated and control products?
  6. Last mile and geospatial: how would you predict delivery time or improve address geocoding accuracy? [Likely] (see [35])
    • What loss would you use if late deliveries cost more than early ones?
  7. Ads: how would you predict click-through rate for sponsored products, and how does calibration interact with the auction? [Likely] (see [3][41])
    • Revenue is flat, CPC is up and conversions are down after a pacing change. How would you diagnose it?
  8. Product categorization and catalog quality: how would you detect and fix wrong attributes (size, color, brand) on listings at catalog scale? [Likely]
  9. Alexa or devices: how would you make an on-device model 10x smaller with minimal quality loss? [Likely] (see [34])
  10. AWS AI services: how would you evaluate whether a new foundation-model fine-tuning method is worth exposing to customers? [Likely]
  11. How would you build a query-understanding model (intent, product type, attributes) for a new marketplace and language with little data? [Likely] (see [36])

O. Applying your own research to Amazon

  1. How would you apply your research or project to a problem at Amazon? [Reported] [18]
    • Which team would benefit most, and what metric would move?
    • What would have to change in your method to work at Amazon's scale and latency?
  2. If you joined this team, what would you work on in your first six months, and how would you measure success? [Likely]
  3. Take your method and tell me the first experiment you would run with our data. [Likely]
    • What result would make you abandon the approach?
  4. How would you explain your project's value to a non-scientist product manager in two minutes? [Likely] (Earn Trust)
  5. Write the one-paragraph "PR/FAQ" headline for a product built on your research. [Likely]

P. LP probes woven into the depth round

  1. Tell me about a time you went deep into the data and found something that changed the direction of the project. [Likely] (Dive Deep; see [1][5])
  2. Describe a decision you made with incomplete information, and how you later validated it. [Reported] [41] (Bias for Action)
  3. Describe a calculated risk you took: what you knew at the time, and how it turned out. [Reported] [42]
  4. Tell me about a time a client or decision maker proposed a plan that was far too aggressive. What did you do? [Reported] [41][42]
  5. Tell me about a time a model you shipped failed or underperformed in production. What did you do? [Likely] (Ownership)
  6. Tell me about a time you disagreed with a senior scientist's technical approach, for example about an evaluation metric, and then committed. [Likely] (see [41][42]; Have Backbone; Disagree and Commit)
  7. Tell me about a time you raised the scientific bar on your team (reviews, standards, reproducibility). [Likely] (Insist on the Highest Standards)
  8. Tell me about a time you learned a new area quickly to solve a problem. [Likely] (Learn and Be Curious)
  9. Tell me about the most ambiguous problem you worked on, and how you scoped it. [Likely]
  10. Tell me about a time you traded scientific purity for customer impact, or refused to. [Likely] (Customer Obsession)
  11. How do you use AI tools in your work? Tell me about a time an AI tool gave you a wrong answer, and how you caught it. [Reported] (Amazon behavioral, 2025-2026) [43]

04. Coding Round for Applied Scientists: DSA Questions Reported at Amazon

Scope: the live-coding (data structures and algorithms) round in the Amazon L5 Applied Scientist (Applied Scientist II) onsite or virtual loop, 2025-2026. Questions only, with no solutions. Tags: [Reported] means a candidate report or prep source ties the question to Amazon (citation [n]). Where the report comes from an SDE, intern or Data Scientist loop, or the source does not name the role, the line says so. [Likely] means a reasoned guess based on the role, team or Amazon's products. No source reported it.

Research note: in this environment the egress proxy blocked Glassdoor, Blind, LeetCode, 1point3acres, Medium, amazon.jobs and the vendor guides, so those sources were read through search-result extracts and not as full pages. The GitHub-hosted sources (the TrueInterview mirrors and ombharatiya question lists, marked "fetched and read in full" in the References) were fetched and read in full. Treat the details of each report as what the candidate said happened, not as verified fact. ML-from-scratch coding (AUC, IoU, k-means, k-NN, attention, softmax, gradient descent and similar) is covered in Chapter 05 and is not repeated here.

Format notes for this round

  • Where coding appears. Amazon's official AS prep page says a technical phone screen comes first, and some teams add a second one [1]. Candidate reports describe the L5 loop as 4 to 6 back-to-back 60-minute interviews: ML breadth, ML depth, live coding, science application or ML system design, and a Bar Raiser [2][3][4][5]. An L5 poster said their recruiter described one coding interview, usually a LeetCode medium, with no coding in the other rounds [4]. One Seattle 2026 report listed "1 Science ML Breadth, 1 Science Depth, 1 Sys Design (Team-specific), 1 coding round and 1 Bar Raiser" [6]. A 2026 aggregator of scientist reports says coding appears in one or two loop rounds, sometimes the hiring manager's, and shares the hour with Leadership Principle (LP) questions [7].
  • Difficulty. Most reports say easy to medium, and often an "easier medium" [4][8][9][10]. Some reports say medium/hard, with "clean production level, optimal code" and edge cases expected [11]. Others got one medium and one hard, with brute force explained before the optimal solution [12], or a LeetCode Medium/Hard such as median of a data stream [13]. One April 2026 AS phone screen set an original dynamic program that was not on any standard list [7][14]. Some AS candidates say they got almost no LeetCode at all (one poster: "roughly 4/5 of the AS I asked") [4][9]. The bar depends on the team and on who interviews you. A Reddit commenter said an SDE interviewer usually gives medium DSA, while an AS interviewer often gives ML or data-science coding [2].
  • Time pressure. LP questions often take the first 20 to 30 minutes of a technical hour [15][16][10]. One 2026 report says LP questions left about 15 minutes for the coding task [7]. Plan to finish a medium in about 25 minutes [10].
  • Environment. Amazon's prep pages ask for syntactically correct code with no pseudo code, written in its Livecode shared editor with no IDE, and they say to check edge cases and bad input [1][17]. Reports say the editor does not run code, so you trace through it by hand [18][10]. Python is the norm for AS candidates, but any language is accepted [19]. The 2026 SDE online assessment added an AI-assisted repository-debugging section in some regions. Live coding rounds still ban AI, and no source reports the AI-assisted section for AS loops [20].
  • What is assessed at L5. Problem framing and clarifying questions, choice of data structure, stating time and space complexity, moving from brute force to optimal, testing and edge cases, and readable, maintainable code [17][19][21][18]. Scientist interviewers also want edge cases and complexity stated without being asked, and they want you to narrate decisions as your own: one candidate who solved course schedule was told too many hints had been given [7][10].
  • Prep signals from candidates. Candidates say Blind 75 or 150 at medium level is usually enough, and that you should know arrays, hash maps, trees, graphs, heaps and basic DP well [22][23][24]. L5 phone screens mix coding, statistics and LPs [25]. Alexa, AGI and LLM teams lean toward ML-implementation coding [26][27]. Amazon AGI phone screens were reported as "standard LC coding plus a backend task" [28]. Amazon SDE-tagged frequency lists (islands, LRU, top-k, intervals) are useful backup practice, because AS coding interviewers are often SDEs [29][30][31][32][20].
  • Reports too vague to list as questions. Several AS reports name only the topic: "implement a data structure from scratch" (4 or 5 small prompts) [2], a sliding-window variant with "rigorous follow-ups" [9], a standard tree medium followed by Transformer questions [2], a DFS exercise and a binary-search question in Senior AS loops [33], a DP problem from the Blind 150 list asked by a hiring manager [2][34], two string DP and hash problems [35], and simple Python data wrangling or a medium SQL question [3][11].
  • Common follow-ups. Scaling to data that does not fit on one machine (MapReduce) [36], streaming versions [14], and "now make it production-grade" [11].

A. Arrays, strings and hashing

  1. [Reported] Given an array of integers and a target, return the indices of the two numbers that add up to the target. AS reports use it as a warm-up before a harder problem [34][7]. A 2026 onsite used an in-flight-movie framing: pick two movie lengths that fit the flight time [37].
    • What changes if the array is sorted, or if there are many queries against the same array?
    • How would you return all unique pairs without duplicates?
    • How would you solve it at scale, when the array does not fit in memory? [14]
  2. [Reported] Find the maximum subarray sum in an integer array. [11][37]
    • How would you also return the start and end indices?
    • What if the array is circular?
  3. [Reported] Compress a string in place by run-length encoding (for example "aabccc" becomes "a2bc3"), and analyze time and space complexity. [6][5]
    • How do you handle counts of 10 or more?
    • What if the compressed string would be longer than the original?
  4. [Reported] For each position in a list, return the product of all other elements. A fraud-detection team screen asked this without allowing division. It was also reported in an April 2026 Amazon phone screen (role not stated). [38][37]
    • How do you handle zeros?
    • Can you do it in O(1) extra space, not counting the output?
  5. [Reported] Find the longest substring without repeating characters. [5][19][37]
    • How would you return the substring itself, not just its length?
  6. [Reported] Divide two integers without using multiplication, division or modulo. [19][14]
    • How do you handle overflow and negative numbers?
  7. [Reported] In a sorted array where every element appears twice except one, find the single element. [19][14]
    • Can you beat O(n)?
  8. [Reported] For each element, find the nearest smaller element to its left. This was reported in an Amazon loop, but the role is not specified. [39]
    • Which data structure gives you amortized O(n)?
  9. [Reported] Merge two sorted arrays in place when the first array has spare capacity at the end. A third-party guide lists it as a sample AS question, and it was reported at Amazon in February 2026. [40][14]
    • What changes if you must merge three sorted arrays and remove duplicates? [14]
  10. [Reported] A hash-map counting problem, followed by: what if the data is too big for one machine? [36]
    • How would you split it into map and reduce stages?
    • How do you handle a skewed key that overloads one reducer?
  11. [Reported] Rotate an n x n matrix by 90 degrees in place. Reported in an AS loop (2024) and in Amazon screens and onsites through May 2026. [41][14][37]
    • How do you rotate counterclockwise, or by 180 degrees, with the same code?
  12. [Reported] Count the subarrays whose sum equals k. This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
    • Why does a sliding window fail when values can be negative?
  13. [Reported] Group anagrams. Reported at Amazon in an August 2026 report and on Amazon-tagged lists (role not stated). [14][20][37]
    • How does your key choice change if the input is Unicode?
  14. [Reported] Find all triplets that sum to zero (3Sum). This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
    • How do you skip duplicate triplets without a set?
  15. [Likely] Given customer reviews as strings, return the k most frequent words, ignoring stop words.
    • How do you break ties?
    • What if the vocabulary is far larger than k?
  16. [Likely] Check whether two strings are one edit apart (insert, delete or replace).
    • How would you generalize this to a full edit-distance threshold k?

B. Intervals and sorting

  1. [Reported] Merge overlapping intervals. Reported in AS loops (Sunnyvale 2020, a June 2026 AS technical screen) and as the coding question in a scientist online assessment. [2][42][43][7][37]
    • Should touching intervals such as [1,3] and [3,5] merge? How does your code change if they should not?
    • How would you insert one new interval into an already merged list?
  2. [Reported] Merge overlapping time ranges for room reservations given as epoch-second pairs. This was tagged Amazon technical screen. [43]
    • How do you handle intervals where start equals end?
  3. [Reported] Given meeting time intervals, find the minimum number of rooms needed. A scientist loop asked it with a min-heap after a two-sum warm-up. [7][37]
    • Can you do it with a sweep line and no heap?
    • How would you also return which meeting goes to which room?
  4. [Reported] Given process start and end times, find the maximum number of processes running at once. Reported at Amazon in July 2026 (role not stated). [14]
    • How would you also return the time window when the peak occurred?
  5. [Reported] Support adding non-overlapping intervals and querying whether a point is covered. This was reported at Amazon (role not stated). [14]
    • Which structure keeps both operations at O(log n)?
  6. [Reported] Given a list of calendar events, find all pairs that conflict. Reported at Amazon in June 2026 (role not stated). [14]
  7. [Likely] Sort a large file of (customer_id, timestamp) records that does not fit in memory.
    • How many passes does external merge sort need, given memory size M?

C. Two pointers and sliding window

  1. [Reported] Design a counter that returns the number of clicks on an ad in the last k minutes. Reported in Amazon ML Engineer and scientist screens; the expected structure was a deque plus a hash map. [7][10][14]
    • How does memory grow with k and with the number of ads?
    • What changes if events can arrive out of order?
  2. [Reported] Find the k closest elements to a target x in a sorted array. [44]
    • Can you do it in O(log n + k)?
  3. [Reported] Return the maximum of every window of size k (sliding window maximum). Reported at Amazon in June 2026 and on Amazon-tagged lists. [14][20]
    • Why does a monotonic deque give O(n)?
  4. [Reported] Find the shortest substring of a string that contains all characters of a pattern (minimum window substring). This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
  5. [Reported] Find the longest substring you can get by replacing at most k characters so that all characters are the same. This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
  6. [Reported] Trapping rain water. Reported in an April 2026 Amazon phone screen and on Amazon-tagged lists. [45][20][37]
    • Can you do it in O(1) extra space?
  7. [Likely] Given daily sales, find the longest run of days whose total is at most a budget B, assuming all values are non-negative.
    • What breaks if negative values are allowed?

D. Linked lists and stacks

  1. [Reported] Swap nodes in pairs (pairwise swap) in a linked list. A Research/Applied Scientist phone screen asked for this as a medium problem in about 25 to 30 minutes. [46][47]
    • Can you write both the recursive and iterative versions?
    • How would you generalize it to reverse every k nodes?
  2. [Reported] Asteroid collision. Reported in an Applied Scientist intern DSA round (Bengaluru, 2026) and in an August 2026 Amazon report. [12][48][14]
    • Which edge cases involve equal sizes or asteroids all moving the same way?
  3. [Reported] Check whether a string of brackets is valid. Reported in Amazon ML Engineer phone screens in 2026. [10][37]
    • How would you return the minimum number of insertions that make it valid?
  4. [Reported] Evaluate an arithmetic expression. A July 2026 Amazon report used postfix notation. [14]
    • How would you extend it to infix with +, -, *, / and parentheses?
  5. [Reported] Merge k sorted lists. This is on Amazon-tagged lists. [45][20]
    • What is the complexity with a heap compared with pairwise merging?
  6. [Reported] Merge k sorted event streams behind an iterator with next() and hasNext(), without loading all events into memory. Reported in a September 2026 Amazon onsite. [14][20]
    • What happens when one stream is much slower than the others?
  7. [Reported] Detect a cycle in a linked list, return where it starts, and break it. Reported at Amazon in June 2026. [14]
  8. [Reported] Copy a linked list where each node has a next pointer and a random pointer. Reported at Amazon in March 2026. [14]
    • Can you do it in O(1) extra space?
  9. [Reported] Implement a stack that pops the most frequent element, breaking ties by most recent (maximum frequency stack). Reported at Amazon in September 2026. [14]

E. Trees and BSTs

  1. [Reported] Given an org tree with salaries, compute the average salary of each manager's direct reports. Reported in Amazon scientist and ML Engineer loops in 2026. [7][10][14]
    • How would you compute the average over all reports, not just direct ones, in one pass?
    • What if the input is a flat list of (employee, manager, salary) rows?
  2. [Reported] Serialize and deserialize a binary tree. Reported at Amazon in February 2026 and on Amazon-tagged lists. [14][20]
    • How does your format handle null children compactly?
  3. [Reported] Find the lowest common ancestor of two nodes in a binary tree. Reported in a February 2026 Amazon phone screen. [20][37]
    • What if one node might not be in the tree?
    • How does it get easier in a BST?
  4. [Reported] Validate a binary search tree. Reported at Amazon in June 2026. [14]
  5. [Reported] Find the kth smallest element in a BST. An August 2026 report included follow-ups. [14]
    • What if the tree is modified often and you need kth smallest many times?
  6. [Reported] Return the right-side view of a binary tree. Reported at Amazon in February 2026. [14]
  7. [Reported] Return the vertical order traversal of a binary tree. Reported at Amazon in June 2026. [14]
  8. [Reported] Build a binary tree from a list of (parent, child, isLeft) descriptions and return its root. Reported at Amazon in August 2026. [14]
  9. [Likely] Given a product category tree (Electronics > Audio > Headphones), return the path from the root to a category and the depth of the deepest leaf.

F. Graphs and grids

  1. [Reported] Count the number of islands in a 2D grid. Reported in AS loops in 2020, 2025 and 2026, in a London 2026 screen, and in Amazon screens with a streaming follow-up in April 2026. [2][12][49][50][14][37]
    • Should diagonals count as connected?
    • What if the grid is streamed row by row and does not fit in memory?
    • Can you solve it with union-find?
  2. [Reported] A BFS problem on a grid (for example, the minimum number of steps from start to target). [51]
    • How do you reconstruct the path?
  3. [Reported] Shortest path with Dijkstra's algorithm where the grid or graph has obstacles. A 2026 source says weighted shortest-path problems are rising at Amazon. [52][20]
    • Why do negative edge weights break Dijkstra?
    • When would A* be the better choice?
  4. [Reported] Explain how you would implement Dijkstra: which data structures, and what runtime? [53]
    • Which data structure gives O((V+E) log V), and which gives O(V^2)?
  5. [Reported] Course schedule: given prerequisite pairs, return a valid order or report a cycle. Reported in 2026 Amazon ML Engineer screens, including a version framed around ads. [10][14][37]
    • How do you return all nodes that are part of a cycle?
  6. [Reported] Given dependent tasks and a task that failed, order the tasks and find which tasks must be rerun. Reported in a September 2026 Amazon onsite. [14]
  7. [Reported] Check whether adding an edge to a directed graph creates a cycle. Reported at Amazon in October 2025. [14]
  8. [Reported] Word ladder (BFS on implicit graph). This is on Amazon-tagged lists. [18][20]
    • How do you build neighbors efficiently using wildcard patterns?
  9. [Reported] Rotting oranges and multi-source BFS: how many minutes until every reachable cell is rotten? Reported at Amazon in April 2026 and on Amazon-tagged lists. [14][20]
  10. [Reported] Search a grid of letters for a word, then for a list of words with a trie (word search I and II). Reported at Amazon in January and February 2026. [14][20]
  11. [Reported] Given equations such as a / b = 2.0, answer division queries (evaluate division). This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
  12. [Reported] Count connected components with union-find. Reported at Amazon in May 2026 (role not stated). [14]
    • How would you use it to group users who share a payment method or address for fraud detection?
  13. [Likely] Clone a graph, for example a knowledge-graph subgraph used by Alexa.
  14. [Likely] Given warehouse aisles as a grid with blocked cells, find the shortest route for a robot from a start to multiple pick locations. This is related to Amazon Robotics.
    • How does the problem change with 2 pick locations compared with k?

G. Heaps, top-k and streaming

  1. [Reported] Find the median of a data stream. Reported as a LeetCode Medium/Hard in an AS loop (Luxembourg, 2023) and at Amazon in January 2026. [13][14]
    • What if most values are small integers in a known range?
    • How would you support a sliding-window median?
  2. [Reported] Top K frequent elements. A September 2026 Amazon report added a streaming follow-up. [18][45][14]
    • Can you compare heap and bucket sort?
    • How would you approximate this with Count-Min Sketch when there are billions of items?
  3. [Reported] K closest points to the origin. This is Amazon SDE-tagged. [45]
    • Can you use quickselect, and what is its worst case?
  4. [Reported] Find the kth largest element in an unsorted array. This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
  5. [Reported] Rearrange a string so that no two adjacent characters are the same, or report that it is impossible. Reported at Amazon in May 2026. [14][20]
  6. [Reported] Given tasks and a cooldown n between identical tasks, find the minimum time to run them all (task scheduler). This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
  7. [Likely] Return the top k products by click-through rate in a sliding one-hour window over a click stream.
    • What state must you keep per product?

H. Binary search

  1. [Reported] Find the minimum of a rotated sorted array. Reported in Amazon scientist and ML Engineer phone screens. [7][10]
    • How do duplicates change the worst case?
    • How would you then search for a target in the same array?
  2. [Reported] Search a 2D matrix whose rows and columns are both sorted, and find the optimal approach. [5]
    • Can you compare the staircase search with binary search on each row?
  3. [Reported] Implement binary search and state its time complexity. [40]
    • Can you write it to find the first and the last occurrence of a value? [20]
  4. [Reported] Koko eating bananas: find the minimum eating speed to finish within h hours. Reported in an April 2026 Amazon phone screen. [14][37]
    • How would you reframe it as "ship packages within D days"?
  5. [Reported] Design a key-value store that keeps a time-stamped history of values and returns the value at a given time. Reported in a March 2026 Amazon onsite and a September 2026 phone screen. [14][37]
    • How would you use it to look up point-in-time feature values for training data?
  6. [Reported] Find the kth smallest element in two sorted arrays. This was reported at Amazon (role not stated). [14]
  7. [Likely] Given a monotonic model-score threshold function, find the smallest threshold that keeps precision at or above a target. This is binary search on the answer.

I. Dynamic programming

  1. [Reported] Batch items into buckets so that total padding cost is as low as possible. This original DP was reported in an April 2026 Applied Scientist phone screen. [7][10][14]
    • What is your DP state, and what is the recurrence?
    • How does the answer change if the number of buckets is fixed?
  2. [Reported] A minimum-coins variant solved with 1-D DP (AS intern, 2025), also reported at Amazon as "minimum bills and coins" in June 2026. [51][14][20]
    • Can you return the actual coins used, not just the count?
    • How do you count the number of ways instead?
  3. [Reported] Edit distance between two strings, with a follow-up variant (July 2026). [14]
    • Can you reduce memory to O(min(m, n))?
  4. [Reported] Word break: can a string be split into dictionary words? A July 2026 report gave a dictionary helper function. [14][20]
    • How does a trie help?
    • How would you return all valid segmentations?
  5. [Reported] House robber and its variants (circular street, binary tree). Reported in Amazon screens and onsites in November 2025. [14][37]
  6. [Reported] Best time to buy and sell stock, including the versions with unlimited trades and at most k trades. Reported at Amazon in March and July 2026. [14][20][37]
  7. [Reported] Decode ways. This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
  8. [Reported] Find the longest palindromic substring. This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
  9. [Reported] Regular expression matching with . and *. This was reported at Amazon (role not stated). [14]
  10. [Likely] Longest increasing subsequence.
    • Can you do it in O(n log n)?
  11. [Likely] Viterbi-style DP: given emission and transition scores, find the highest-scoring tag sequence.

J. Randomization and sampling

  1. [Reported] Random pick with weight: build a weighted random number generator from a probability array. Reported in AS and Senior AS screens in 2026. [33][5][54][7][10]
    • How do you handle all-zero weights or a single element?
    • What does the alias method give you, and what does it cost?
  2. [Reported] Design a set with insert, delete and getRandom in O(1). This is on an Amazon-tagged list. [20]
    • How do you support duplicates?
  3. [Likely] Reservoir sampling: choose k items uniformly from a stream of unknown length.
    • What changes for weighted reservoir sampling?
  4. [Likely] Shuffle an array uniformly at random (Fisher-Yates), and explain why the naive swap-with-any-index version is biased.

K. Design-style data structures

  1. [Reported] Implement an LRU cache. Reported in Amazon loops, including a basic-DS AS-adjacent report and an April 2026 phone screen. [49][18][37]
    • Can every operation be O(1)?
    • How would you make it thread-safe?
    • What about LFU, or TTL-based eviction? [20]
  2. [Reported] Implement a trie that supports adding words and searching with a wildcard character. This was reported at Amazon, and autocomplete design is on an Amazon-tagged list. [14][20]
    • How would you return the top-3 completions by query frequency?
  3. [Reported] Given a stream of (timestamp, message), decide whether each message may be printed, so that the same message prints at most once every 10 seconds. This was reported for Amazon new grads in August 2026. [20]
  4. [Likely] Implement a min-stack or a max-queue that supports O(1) min or max.

L. Practical data-processing algorithms

  1. [Reported] Given order events and ad-impression events, find for each user the pair with the smallest time difference. Reported at Amazon in February 2026. [14]
    • What is the complexity if each list is sorted by time?
  2. [Reported] Given an Audible reading-event stream, flag books that receive abusive activity. Reported at Amazon in February 2026. [14]
  3. [Reported] Parse log lines and aggregate them by a key (group-by). Reported at Amazon in March 2026. [14]
    • What if the logs do not fit in memory?
  4. [Reported] Given send and receive CSV logs, compute each message's latency. Reported at Amazon in May 2026. [14]
    • How do you handle a receive with no matching send?
  5. [Reported] Return the top k slowest requests from a set of logs. This was reported at Amazon (role not stated). [14]
  6. [Likely] Given a log of (user_id, session_start, session_end), compute each user's total active time, merging overlapping sessions.
