05. ML Coding: Implementing Algorithms and Metrics from Scratch

Scope: the "ML coding" variant of the coding round in the Amazon L5 Applied Scientist (Applied Scientist II) onsite or virtual loop, 2025-2026. This round asks you to implement an ML algorithm, metric, loss or training loop in plain Python, NumPy or (on LLM teams) PyTorch, usually with no scikit-learn or high-level library calls. Questions only, with no solutions.

Tags: [Reported] means a first-person candidate report, or an aggregator that compiles candidate reports, ties this coding task to an Amazon science or ML loop (citation [n]). Where the source is an aggregator or a vendor-published debrief, the reference list says so. [Likely] means an inference from the role, Amazon's teams and products, or a concept that was reported in a breadth or depth round, turned into a coding task. A [Likely] item was not reported as a coding question. Items listed only by a vendor prep guide, a prep blog or a practice site, and not tied to a candidate report, are tagged [Likely] with the source noted.

Research note (read this): in the review pass for this chapter (October 2026), web search was unavailable and the egress proxy refused every non-GitHub host, so the 38 references carried over from the first draft could not be re-fetched. They were not removed, because a refused connection is not evidence that a page is dead; they reflect what the chapter 01-04 research recorded, mostly from search-result extracts. The new [Reported] items below come from GitHub-hosted aggregators that were fetched and read in full during this pass [39]-[43]. Two of those pages cover Amazon's Research Scientist and Machine Learning Engineer tracks, which share the science loop format with Applied Scientist; each item says which track it came from. Treat what each source describes as the candidate's account of what happened.

Format notes for this round

  • Where it sits in the loop. Amazon's official AS prep page describes the onsite as four 55-minute interviews with members of the science community. Candidates for AS II usually report 5 rounds: ML breadth, ML depth, coding, science application or system design, and a Bar Raiser [1][3][11]. Most L5 loops have one coding round, sometimes two, and the hiring manager's round may add a short coding task [40][41]. Depending on the team and the interviewer, it can be LeetCode-style DSA, data manipulation in pandas or NumPy, or ML from scratch [11][12][13]. One Reddit-sourced comment, summarized on Glassdoor, says SDE interviewers tend to give medium DSA, while AS interviewers often give ML or data-science coding [6]. One aggregator says some applied-science loops had no algorithm problems at all, only hand-written sigmoid, softmax and attention during the technical discussion [41].
  • Which teams lean toward ML coding. Reports from Alexa, AGI and LLM teams lean toward implementing ML from scratch [24][25]. Amazon AGI reports include "debug a broken Transformer implementation", "build/train a small classifier live" and "reinforcement learning fundamentals + coding" [43]. A Blind loop summary lists "implement a simple ML algorithm or training loop from scratch (e.g., logistic regression with gradient descent, k-means)" as one coding format [12]. Vendor guides say libraries are banned so the interviewer can test whether you truly understand the method [19][21]; an Amazon online-assessment softmax task explicitly says "do not rely on any third-party libraries" [42].
  • Environment. You code in Amazon's shared editor (Livecode), with no IDE and usually no way to run the code. Amazon expects syntactically correct code, not pseudo code, and expects you to check edge cases [1][2][30]. Python is the default; NumPy and PyTorch are expected for numerical tasks [40].
  • Bar at L5. You are expected to write correct, readable, vectorized code. Aggregated reports list what interviewers check: numerical stability (subtract the max before exponentiating, stable variance formulas), vectorization when asked, linking statistics to their maximum-likelihood estimators, and edge cases such as all-zero weights or a single element, stated without prompting [40][41]. Expect follow-ups that scale the solution up (batching, streaming, distributed) or tie it to an Amazon use case.
  • Time pressure from LPs. Each technical round typically opens with Leadership Principle (LP) questions, often 20-30 minutes. Several reports describe only about 15 minutes left for the coding task after long LP answers, and a rejection that followed [1][21][40][41]. Keep each LP story near five minutes.
  • Typical pacing (inferred). About 5 minutes to clarify the task and the input/output contract, 15-30 minutes to implement depending on how long LPs ran, 5 minutes on tests and complexity, then extensions. Not every report confirms this split.

A. Evaluation metrics from scratch

A1. Classification metrics

  1. Write a program that computes ROC-AUC from a list of scores and binary labels. [Reported] [3][4][5][17]
    • How do you handle tied scores?
    • Can you do it in O(n log n) using ranks (Mann-Whitney U) instead of building the full ROC curve?
    • What do you return if all labels are the same class?
    • How would you compute AUC over a dataset too large for one machine, for example with a fixed-bin histogram of scores per class?
  2. Compute precision, recall and F1 from predicted and true labels, without libraries. [Likely] (metric choice was reported in breadth and depth rounds [21]; precision, recall and AUC for imbalanced fraud data were reported in Amazon science screens [40])
    • How do you extend it to multi-class with macro, micro and weighted averaging?
    • What should the function return when there are no predicted positives?
  3. Build a confusion matrix for K classes from two integer arrays, vectorized in NumPy. [Likely]
  4. Given scores and labels, find the threshold that maximizes F1, or that maximizes recall while precision stays at or above a target. [Likely]
    • Can you do it in a single pass after sorting?
  5. Compute the precision-recall curve and the average precision (PR-AUC). [Likely]
    • Why can ROC-AUC look optimistic under heavy class imbalance, for example fraud or abuse detection?
  6. Implement binary and multi-class log loss (cross-entropy) with numerically safe clipping. [Likely] (the cross-entropy versus MSE question was reported in breadth [7])
  7. Compute expected calibration error (ECE) with equal-width bins, and the data for a reliability diagram. [Likely] (ad click-through-rate (CTR) prediction and auction teams)
    • Equal-width or equal-mass bins: which would you use, and why?
  8. Fit temperature scaling for a trained classifier: find the single scalar T that minimizes validation negative log-likelihood, by gradient descent or a 1-D search, and apply it to the logits. [Likely] (temperature scaling is named among the "numerical care" points interviewers look for [41])
    • Why does temperature scaling leave accuracy and AUC unchanged?
  9. Implement isotonic regression with the pool-adjacent-violators algorithm to calibrate CTR scores. [Likely] (ads calibration)
    • When would you prefer it to Platt or temperature scaling, and what is its overfitting risk?

A2. Ranking and retrieval metrics

  1. Compute precision@k, recall@k and NDCG@k for a ranked list of search results with graded relevance. [Likely] (Amazon search, ads and recommendations)
    • What is IDCG, and how do you handle queries with no relevant items?
    • How do you average across queries?
  2. Compute mean reciprocal rank (MRR) and mean average precision (MAP) for a batch of queries. [Likely]
  3. Given impressions with click logs, compute CTR per position, and suggest a simple correction for position bias. [Likely] (position bias in clickstream relevance was reported in a depth round)

A3. Vision, NLP and forecasting metrics

  1. Write a function that computes Intersection over Union (IoU) for two bounding boxes. [Likely] (listed as an Amazon Senior AS coding question by a vendor guide [19], not by a candidate report)
    • What edge cases come up when boxes do not overlap or have zero area?
    • How would you vectorize it for N x M boxes?
  2. Implement non-maximum suppression (NMS) using your IoU function. [Likely] (vision teams such as Lab126, Amazon Go and robotics)
    • What is the complexity, and how would you speed it up?
  3. Compute mean average precision (mAP) at an IoU threshold for an object detector over a dataset. [Likely]
  4. Implement BLEU (n-gram precision with brevity penalty) or ROUGE-L for a candidate and a reference. [Likely] (Alexa, translation and LLM teams)
  5. Compute perplexity from per-token log-probabilities, handling padding masks. [Likely]
  6. Compute word error rate (WER) with edit distance between a hypothesis and a reference transcript. [Likely] (Alexa ASR; accented-speech ASR was reported as a science-application problem)
    • How would you also return the counts of substitutions, insertions and deletions?
  7. Implement forecasting metrics: MAPE, sMAPE, and weighted quantile (pinball) loss at P50 and P90. [Likely] (Amazon supply-chain forecasting teams)
    • What goes wrong with MAPE when actuals are zero?

B. Clustering, neighbors and similarity search

  1. Implement k-means from scratch in Python or NumPy. [Reported] [12][16][17][39] (the aggregator listing records a December 2025 Amazon sighting of "Implement K-means and solve interval/frequency tasks" [39])
    • How do you initialize centroids? Implement k-means++ seeding as a separate function.
    • When should it stop, and what do you do with an empty cluster?
    • What is the per-iteration complexity? Can you vectorize the distance computation?
    • How would you choose k?
    • How would you run it on 1 billion product embeddings (mini-batch, distributed)?
  2. Implement k-nearest neighbors classification from scratch. [Likely] (titled as an Amazon ML Scientist question by a prep blog [22], not a verified candidate report; kNN appeared in an AS II breadth report [38])
    • How do you choose k and break ties in voting?
    • Why must features be scaled, and how do you avoid leaking test statistics into the scaling?
    • What is the query cost, and how does it change in high dimensions?
    • Extend it to distance-weighted kNN regression.
  3. Compute the Chamfer distance between two point clouds efficiently, using an exact nearest-neighbor structure such as a KD-tree instead of computing all pairwise distances. [Likely] (Amazon-framed practice question on a prep site [18], not a candidate report)
    • How does a KD-tree perform as dimensionality grows?
  4. Build a KD-tree and run a nearest-neighbor query. [Likely] (follow-up to the Chamfer task)
  5. Given a query embedding and a matrix of N item embeddings, return the top-k items by cosine similarity, using a heap rather than a full sort. [Likely] (semantic search, Rufus retrieval [33])
    • What is the complexity of the full sort versus the heap?
    • What changes for 100 million items (approximate nearest neighbor: IVF, HNSW, product quantization)?
  6. Implement random-hyperplane locality-sensitive hashing for near-duplicate product detection. [Likely]
  7. Compute pairwise Euclidean distances between two matrices without Python loops, using the expansion ||a||^2 - 2ab + ||b||^2. [Likely] (core subroutine of the reported k-means task)
    • What numerical issue can produce small negative values, and how do you fix it?
  8. Implement greedy maximal marginal relevance (MMR) re-ranking: from N candidates with relevance scores and embeddings, pick k items that trade off relevance against similarity to items already picked. [Likely] (Amazon Science's MUSS work targets relevance-plus-diversity subset selection for recommendations [44])
    • What is the complexity of the greedy loop, and how would you scale it to millions of candidates?

C. Linear models, optimization and estimation

  1. Implement a simple ML training loop from scratch, for example logistic regression with gradient descent. [Reported] [12]
    • Derive the gradient of the log loss before you code it.
    • How do you add L2 (and L1) regularization? Should the bias be regularized?
    • How do you avoid overflow in the sigmoid?
    • How do you check convergence?
  2. Write gradient descent without libraries, and explain each line. [Reported] [21][39] (vendor-published debrief [21]; "Write and explain gradient descent pseudocode" in an Amazon aggregator listing, undated [39])
    • How would you change it to mini-batch SGD with momentum?
  3. Code a traditional ML classifier from scratch, or build and train a small classifier live (the candidate chose the model). [Reported] [8][43] (the live-classifier task is from an Amazon AGI aggregator entry [43])
    • Why did you choose that model, and what are its assumptions?
    • How do you show the interviewer it is learning, with no plotting tools?
  4. Implement linear regression with the closed-form normal equation and with gradient descent, and compare them. [Likely] (closed form versus GD and OLS were reported in breadth [13][23]; least-squares assumptions were reported in Amazon science screens [40])
    • When is the matrix X^T X singular, and what do you do about it (ridge, pseudo-inverse)?
  5. Implement multinomial (softmax) regression for K classes with a vectorized gradient. [Likely]
  6. Implement the Adam optimizer update for a parameter vector. [Likely] (Adam versus momentum SGD, and Adam versus AdamW, were reported in Amazon screens and depth rounds [40][41])
    • Why is bias correction needed in the early steps?
    • Change it to AdamW: where does decoupled weight decay enter the update, and why does it differ from L2 in the loss?
  7. Implement a numerical gradient check to verify an analytic gradient. [Likely]
  8. Implement L1-regularized regression with proximal gradient (soft-thresholding) or coordinate descent. [Likely] (L1 versus L2 sparsity was reported in breadth [10][16])
  9. Implement a Newton-Raphson (IRLS) step for logistic regression. [Likely]
    • When does Newton's method beat gradient descent, and what does it cost per step?
  10. Implement stratified k-fold cross-validation and use it to tune the regularization strength of your logistic regression. [Likely]
  11. Write a NumPy function for the mean and variance of an array, then relate them to maximum-likelihood estimation. [Reported] [39][40][41] (Research Scientist and MLE loops; aggregator sighting dated September 2026 [39])
    • Why do the sample variance and the Gaussian MLE of the variance differ, and which is biased?
    • How do you compute variance in a numerically stable way, and along a chosen axis without loops?
  12. Write the negative log-likelihood of a Bernoulli model and of a Gaussian model in code, and show that they reduce to log loss and squared error. [Likely] (squared loss as a Gaussian likelihood and log loss from the Bernoulli likelihood were reported in Amazon science screens [40])
  13. Implement Welford's online algorithm for a running mean and variance over a stream, and merge the statistics from two shards. [Likely] (follow-up to item 38; "stable variance formulas" are named as an evaluation point [40])

D. Trees and ensembles

  1. Implement the split search for a decision-tree node: find the feature and threshold that maximize information gain or Gini reduction. [Likely] (how splits are chosen was reported in breadth [36])
    • What is the complexity per node, and how does pre-sorting help?
    • How do you handle categorical features and missing values?
  2. Implement a full decision-tree classifier with recursive building, max-depth and min-samples stopping rules, and predict. [Likely]
  3. Implement prediction for a trained tree stored as arrays of nodes (feature, threshold, left, right, value). [Likely]
  4. Implement a random forest on top of your tree: bootstrap sampling, feature subsampling and majority voting. [Likely] (bagging versus boosting is the most-reported breadth topic)
    • How would you compute out-of-bag error?
  5. Implement gradient boosting for regression with depth-1 stumps and squared loss. [Likely]
    • What changes for log loss, and what are the "pseudo-residuals"?
  6. Compute permutation feature importance for any trained model. [Likely] (impurity versus permutation importance was reported in Amazon science screens [40])

E. Neural networks and backpropagation

  1. Implement forward and backward passes for a 2-layer MLP in NumPy, and train it on a toy dataset. [Likely] (backpropagation for an MLP was reported in breadth [35])
    • What are the shapes of every gradient?
    • How would you initialize the weights, and why?
  2. Implement a numerically stable softmax with no third-party libraries, then softmax-plus-cross-entropy with its gradient with respect to the logits. [Reported] [39][41][42] (an Amazon online-assessment task requires the max-subtraction form for inputs in [-1000, 1000] [42]; hand-written softmax was also reported in AS loops [41])
    • Why does the combined gradient simplify to p - y?
  3. Implement sigmoid by hand without overflow for large negative inputs, then ReLU, tanh and GELU with their derivatives. [Reported] (sigmoid [39][41]); the other activations are [Likely]
  4. Implement dropout (inverted dropout) for training and inference. [Likely]
  5. Implement the batch normalization forward pass with running statistics, and explain or implement the backward pass. [Likely] (batch norm was reported in breadth)
    • How does it behave differently at train and inference time?
  6. Implement a 2D convolution (single channel, then multi-channel) with stride and padding, using loops and then im2col. [Likely] (vision teams; convolution and receptive fields were reported in breadth [41])
  7. Implement a single LSTM or GRU cell forward step in matrix form. [Likely] (LSTM math and recurrent shapes in matrix form were reported in breadth [26][32])
    • How does backpropagation through time work, and where do vanishing gradients come from?
  8. Build a minimal scalar autograd engine (a Value class with +, *, tanh and backward via topological sort). [Likely]
  9. Implement a PyTorch training loop for a small model: data loader, forward, loss, backward, optimizer step, evaluation. [Reported] [39] (Amazon aggregator listing, undated; "practice a training loop from memory" is advised for the coding round [41])
    • Where do zero_grad, model.train() and model.eval() go, and what breaks if you forget each?
    • Add gradient accumulation and gradient clipping by global norm.
    • Add early stopping on validation loss and a warmup-plus-cosine learning-rate schedule.
  10. Implement focal loss for binary classification and its gradient. [Likely] (class imbalance was reported in depth rounds [21])
    • How do the alpha and gamma parameters change the loss for easy and hard examples?
  11. Implement cross-entropy with label smoothing. [Likely]
  12. Implement symmetric per-channel int8 quantization of a weight matrix, dequantize it, and measure the error on a matrix multiply. [Likely] (Amazon Science publishes low-precision LLM training work [45])
    • Why does per-channel scaling beat per-tensor scaling when a few weights are outliers?

F. Attention, transformers and LLM components

  1. Implement scaled dot-product attention by hand in NumPy or PyTorch. [Reported] [39][41] (hand-written attention reported in AS loops, aggregator sighting dated July 2026 [39]; attention "at root level" and the math of self-attention were also reported in depth and breadth rounds [27][29])
    • Why divide by sqrt(d_k)?
    • What changes if Wq = Wk = Wv? (reported as a breadth question [7])
  2. Add a causal mask and a padding mask to your attention function. [Likely]
    • Where exactly is the mask applied, and why use -inf instead of 0?
  3. Implement multi-head self-attention, including the reshape, split heads and output projection. [Likely]
    • What is the time and memory complexity in sequence length?
  4. Implement sinusoidal positional encoding, or rotary embeddings (RoPE). [Likely] (writing the RoPE formula was reported in Amazon science depth rounds [40][41])
  5. Implement layer normalization and RMSNorm. [Likely]
    • Pre-norm or post-norm: what is the difference in training stability?
  6. Implement a decoder step with a KV cache: given cached K and V and a new token, return the output and the updated cache. [Likely] (KV caching and decoder-only inference were reported in a 2026 breadth round [6])
    • How much memory does the cache use for a given model size and context length?
    • How would multi-query or grouped-query attention change your code? (grouped-query attention was reported in screens [40])
  7. Implement a sliding-window (local) attention mask, as in Longformer. [Likely] (Longformer and long-context attention were reported [6])
  8. Implement beam search decoding over a toy next-token probability function. [Likely] (beam search decoding was reported as a conceptual question for an LLM-team AS II [28])
    • How do you handle end-of-sequence tokens and length normalization?
  9. Implement temperature, top-k and top-p (nucleus) sampling from a logits vector. [Likely]
  10. Implement byte-pair encoding (BPE): learn merges on a small corpus, then tokenize a new word. [Likely]
  11. Implement a LoRA linear layer forward pass and explain which parameters are trained. [Likely] (LoRA was reported in AWS AS breadth [4])
    • How do you merge the adapter weights for inference?
  12. Implement the SFT loss for a causal LM, masking prompt tokens so only response tokens count. [Likely] (SFT pipelines were reported in a depth round [21])
  13. Implement the DPO loss given log-probabilities from the policy and reference models for chosen and rejected responses. [Likely] (Alexa and AGI job descriptions mention SFT, DPO and RLHF [37])
  14. Implement a numerically stable, symmetric InfoNCE loss in PyTorch for a batch of paired image and text embeddings (image-to-text and text-to-image). [Reported] [39][40][41] (Research Scientist and MLE loops; aggregator sighting dated February 2026 [39])
    • Where does the temperature go, and why use log-sum-exp in both directions?
    • Why are in-batch negatives a biased sample, and how does batch size change the loss?
  15. You are handed a Transformer implementation that trains but performs badly. Find and fix the bugs. [Reported] [43] (Amazon AGI, aggregator; underlying reports not individually named)
    • Which bugs would you check first (mask direction, missing scaling, wrong softmax axis, residual or norm placement, positional encoding added twice)?
    • What small sanity tests (overfit one batch, compare with a reference shape) would you run?
  16. Implement attention with an online (streaming) softmax over key blocks, keeping a running max and running sum, so the full N x N score matrix is never stored. [Likely] (flash attention was reported in Amazon depth rounds [41])
    • Show that your blocked result matches the naive result.
  17. Implement a mixture-of-experts layer with top-k gating and an auxiliary load-balancing loss. [Likely] (dense versus mixture-of-experts was reported in an Amazon "LLM depth" round; see chapter 07)
    • What happens to the experts if you drop the load-balancing term?
  18. Implement the acceptance step of speculative decoding: given draft and target probabilities for k draft tokens, decide how many to accept and sample the correction token. [Likely] (inference-optimization questions on LLM teams)
  19. Implement the PPO clipped surrogate loss given log-probabilities, old log-probabilities and advantages. [Likely] (PPO's clipped objective was reported in Amazon depth rounds [41])
  20. Given G sampled responses per prompt with scalar rewards, compute GRPO group-relative advantages and the policy loss. [Likely] (why GRPO drops the critic, and how to design its reward, were reported in Amazon science screens [40][41])
    • What happens when every response in a group gets the same reward?
  21. Code a reinforcement-learning fundamental from scratch, for example tabular Q-learning on a grid world or REINFORCE with a baseline. [Reported] (Amazon AGI, aggregator: "reinforcement learning fundamentals + coding" [43]); the specific algorithm is [Likely]
  22. Fit a Bradley-Terry model from pairwise preference judgments (for example LLM-judge or human A/B comparisons between model outputs) to get a score per model. [Likely] (LLM evaluation was reported in 2025-2026 science-application rounds; see chapter 07)
  23. Implement a triplet or margin ranking loss with in-batch hard-negative mining for a product-retrieval embedding model. [Likely]

G. Probability, sampling and statistics in code

  1. Build a weighted random number generator from an array of weights (random pick with weight). [Reported] [9][39][40][41] (Senior AS screen, 2026 [9]; Research Scientist and MLE loops, aggregator sighting dated April 2026 [39])
    • What is the complexity per sample with prefix sums plus binary search? Can you do O(1) with the alias method?
    • What should happen if all weights are zero or there is a single element?
  2. Implement reservoir sampling to pick k items uniformly from a stream of unknown length. [Likely]
    • How would you make it weighted?
  3. Implement bootstrap sampling for the mean and its confidence interval, then remove the explicit Python loop. [Reported] [39][40] (Research Scientist and MLE screens; aggregator sighting dated August 2026 [39])
    • Which statistics does the bootstrap estimate poorly?
    • Extend it to a confidence interval for the AUC difference between two models scored on the same examples.
  4. Implement a two-sample t-test (Welch) or a two-proportion z-test for an A/B test, from scratch. [Likely] (A/B testing, p-values and power were reported in breadth and screens [40])
    • How would you compute the required sample size for a given minimum detectable effect?
  5. Implement a Bayesian (Naive Bayes) classifier from scratch, with Laplace smoothing and log-space computation, then apply it to scenarios the interviewer gives. [Reported] [40][41] (Research Scientist and MLE loops, sometimes in the hiring manager's round; Naive Bayes also appeared in an AS II breadth report [38])
  6. Implement PCA via SVD, or via eigendecomposition of the covariance matrix, and project data onto the top k components. [Likely] (PCA and covariance were reported in breadth)
    • How would you implement power iteration for the top component only?
  7. Implement EM for a 1-D Gaussian mixture model with two components. [Likely]
  8. Implement Thompson sampling or UCB for a multi-armed bandit over ad creatives or widgets, and simulate regret. [Likely] (ads and personalization)

H. Recommendation, search and NLP building blocks

  1. Implement matrix factorization for implicit or explicit ratings with SGD or ALS. [Likely] (Amazon recommender-systems roles list collaborative filtering [34])
    • How do you handle cold-start users and items?
  2. Implement item-to-item collaborative filtering: compute cosine similarity between item vectors from a sparse user-item matrix, and recommend the top N. [Likely] (Amazon's well-known item-to-item recommendations)
    • How would you scale this computation to millions of items?
  3. Implement TF-IDF and BM25 scoring for a query against a small document collection. [Likely] (product search)
  4. Implement a pointwise or pairwise learning-to-rank loss (for example, RankNet) and its gradient. [Likely]
  5. Implement n-gram language-model counting with add-k smoothing, and score a sentence. [Likely]
  6. Implement a simple word2vec skip-gram negative-sampling update for one (center, context) pair. [Likely]
  7. Deduplicate near-identical product titles using normalized string keys or character shingles plus Jaccard similarity. [Likely]

I. Data manipulation and ML pipeline coding

  1. Compute a rolling average when some dates are missing. [Reported] [11]
    • Should missing days count as zero or be skipped? Defend your choice.
  2. Load a dataset in pandas, clean it, engineer features, and fit a model, explaining each step. [Reported] [13][31]
    • Which steps can leak information from the test set?
  3. Answer simple data-wrangling questions in Python (group-by, joins, aggregations) alongside standard DSA. [Reported] [3]
  4. Return the top-K most frequent elements in an array, then in a stream. [Reported] [21][39] (aggregator sighting of a streaming follow-up variant dated September 2026 [39])
  5. Given a company hierarchy as a tree with salaries, compute the average salary of each manager's direct reports. [Reported] [40][41] (Research Scientist and MLE loop coding)
    • What do you return for a manager with no direct reports?
  6. Count an ad's clicks in the last k minutes from a stream of click events, using a deque and a hash map. [Reported] [40][41] (MLE and Research Scientist screens)
    • How does memory grow with the number of ads, and how would you expire idle ads?
  7. Split an ordered list of sequences into contiguous batches, where each batch costs a fixed overhead plus padding up to its longest item, so total cost is minimized. Give the DP state, the recurrence and a worked example. [Reported] [39][40] (Applied Scientist phone screen, aggregator sighting dated April 2026)
    • What is the complexity, and can you bound the batch size to speed it up?
    • How would a greedy bucketing heuristic compare in practice for an inference server?
  8. Implement a train, validation and test split that is stratified by label and grouped by user, so no user appears in two splits. [Likely]
  9. Implement a time-based split and an expanding-window backtest for a demand-forecasting model. [Likely] (supply-chain forecasting)
  10. Implement standard scaling and one-hot encoding as fit/transform classes that handle unseen categories. [Likely]
  11. Implement mini-batch iteration over a dataset that does not fit in memory, with shuffling. [Likely]
  12. Implement random oversampling, or class-weighted loss, for an imbalanced dataset, and show the effect on your metric function. [Likely] (class imbalance was reported in depth rounds [21])
  13. Simulate data-parallel training: given gradient arrays from N workers, implement a ring all-reduce (reduce-scatter then all-gather) and check that it matches a plain average. [Likely] (inter-node communication and pipeline-parallel schedules were reported in the most specialized Amazon screens [41])
    • How much data does each worker send, as a function of N and the gradient size?

J. Round-level probes (how the interviewer pushes at L5)

  1. Before writing code: what are the inputs, outputs, data types and sizes? What should happen on empty or degenerate input? [Likely] (Amazon's prep pages stress edge cases and bad input [1][2])
  2. What is the time and space complexity of your implementation, and where is the bottleneck? [Likely]
  3. You cannot run the code. Walk me through it on a tiny example by hand. [Likely] (third-party guides note that code is usually not executed [30]; aggregators say coding is "discussed rather than run" in screens [40])
  4. How would you test this function? Which unit tests and invariants would you write? [Likely]
  5. How would this change if the data were 1000x larger, or arrived as a stream? [Likely]
  6. Now apply it to our team's problem. For example, where would k-means or kNN fit in product categorization or Rufus retrieval? [Likely]
  7. Estimate the compute time and memory-transfer time of a residual MLP block at batch size 1 and batch size 256, given memory bandwidth, on-chip memory and peak throughput. Which case is memory-bound? [Reported] [39][40][41] (manager-level screen, aggregator sighting dated August 2026)
  8. Tell me about a time you dove deep into data or code to find a root cause (an LP question opening or closing the coding round). [Reported] [21]

Prep priorities (derived from the tags above)

  • Most-reported ML coding tasks: AUC from scores and labels (1), k-means (20), logistic regression or a gradient-descent training loop (28, 29, 55), a classifier from scratch or trained live (30), stable softmax, sigmoid and attention by hand (48, 49, 59), stable symmetric InfoNCE (72), NumPy mean and variance with MLE (38), bootstrap CI (84), a Bayesian classifier (86) and weighted random pick (82).
  • Team-specific: AGI and LLM teams add debugging a broken Transformer (73) and RL coding (79); the [Likely] LLM items (60-71, 74-78) are the natural follow-ups.
  • Do not neglect: data-manipulation and ML-flavored DSA coding (97-109), including the batching DP (103). It shows up in screens and loops where the interviewer is a scientist, not an SDE.
  • Time management: practice each core primitive so it fits in 15 minutes, because long LP answers squeeze the coding time [40][41].

06. ML and Science System Design: Recommendations, Search, Ads, Fraud, Forecasting, Alexa/Rufus LLMs, Pricing and Supply Chain

Scope: the "Science Application" / "ML System Design (team-specific)" round in the Amazon L5 Applied Scientist (Applied Scientist II) onsite loop, 2025-2026. This chapter is questions only, with no answers.

Research note (please read): no web searches or page fetches worked for this chapter. The run's shared web-search budget was already used up, and direct fetches to Glassdoor, Blind, Medium, Dataford, IGotAnOffer, amazon.jobs and similar sites were refused by this environment's egress proxy. Every [Reported] tag below therefore comes from the sourced findings already gathered for chapters 01-04 of this guide. Those findings were themselves built from search-result extracts of the cited pages, not full reads. Each [Reported] question cites the original page. A question is tagged [Reported] only if one of those chapters attributes it to an Amazon science interview. Everything else is [Likely]. A follow-up session with a fresh search budget could add more first-person design-round reports, especially from Blind and 1point3acres.

Review pass (2026-10-14): an adversarial review tried to re-verify the references and found none reachable. WebFetch could not resolve medium.com, leetcode.com, dataford.io or igotanoffer.com. The proxy returned 403 for amazon.jobs, amazon.science, spectrum.ieee.org, Glassdoor and Blind. The web-search budget was also used up. No reference could be confirmed live or confirmed dead, so none were removed. The three amazon.jobs job IDs [28][29][30] are especially unverified, because postings expire. The review removed duplicate and generic items and added a few [Likely] questions. It added no new [Reported] items, because none could be sourced.

Format notes for this round

  • Where it sits in the loop. L5 AS loops are usually 4-6 interviews of 55-60 minutes each. One 2025-2026 Applied Scientist II report gave the loop as "1 Science ML Breadth, 1 Science Depth, 1 Sys Design (Team-specific), 1 coding round and 1 Bar Raiser" [2]. Amazon's official prep page describes the loop as about four 55-minute interviews with members of the science community. It says interviewers are assigned competencies based on the Leadership Principles and typically ask 2-3 behavioral questions each [1]. Other candidates and guides list "ML application" or "science application" as a distinct onsite round [17][18].
  • L5 versus L4. Blind commenters say L5 loops usually include the design or application round and L4 loops may not [5]. The L5 expectation is end-to-end ownership: framing a vague business problem, choosing data, labels and models, defining offline and online metrics, and launching and monitoring the system, with sound trade-off calls along the way [5][16].
  • Who runs it. It is often the hiring manager or a senior scientist from the hiring team, and the problem is drawn from that team's domain [2][8]. Examples include Ads trust [9], Alexa speech [10], product search and recommendations [3][12][13], and LLM agents and RAG [3][14].
  • What is assessed. IGotAnOffer describes the round as being given a high-level ML problem and proposing the data sources, annotation strategy, modeling technique, evaluation metrics, and trade-offs and risks [7]. In candidate reports, interviewers drill into the data pipeline "from collection to deployment" [9], dataset construction and sampling [11], and bias in implicit feedback such as click position bias [12][13]. In 2025-2026, GenAI design prompts appear more often: agentic recommenders, RAG and LLM-agent evaluation [3][6][14].
  • Leadership Principles. Expect 1-2 LP questions inside this round, often Customer Obsession, Dive Deep, Invent and Simplify, or Think Big. Reports put LPs at roughly 5-15 minutes of each technical interview [1][16].
  • How it differs from SDE system design. The weight is on the science (problem framing, labels, modeling, metrics, experimentation) more than on distributed-systems boxes. Candidates still report questions about scale, latency and deployment [9][22]. One campus loop had a separate "Scalable ML" round [21].

Tag legend: [Reported] means a cited source attributes the question, or a close paraphrase, to an Amazon science interview (AS unless noted). [Likely] means a grounded inference from the team's products, job postings or Amazon Science publications. It has not been seen verbatim.

A. Generic framing (the questions every design round opens or pivots with)

  1. Here is a high-level ML problem from our team. Propose a solution, covering data sources, annotation strategy, modeling techniques, evaluation metrics, and trade-offs and risks. [Reported] [7]
    • Which parts of the problem actually need ML, and which could be rules or heuristics?
    • What is your v1 that could ship in one quarter, and what is v2?
  2. Walk me through the full data pipeline for your design, from collection to deployment. [Reported] [9]
    • Where do labels come from, and how noisy are they?
    • How do you keep training and serving features consistent?
  3. Pick an agentic product we could build. Who is the customer, and how do you measure success? (Seattle 2026 report) [Reported] [2]
    • What would the press release headline say (Working Backwards)?
    • Which single metric would you defend to a VP?
  4. How would you take this from a prototype to Amazon-scale traffic? [Likely]
    • Where do latency and cost hit first: feature fetch, model inference, or candidate generation?
    • What do you precompute offline, and what must be computed in real time?
  5. What offline metric and online metric would you pair, and what do you do when they disagree? [Likely]
    • How do you design the A/B test, including the unit of randomization, duration, power and guardrails?
  6. What would make you kill or pivot this project after launch? [Likely]

B. Recommendations and personalization

  1. Design a pipeline to improve personalized product recommendations on the Amazon homepage. [Reported] [6]
    • What is the north-star metric, and what are the guardrails?
    • How do you split the work into candidate generation, ranking and re-ranking?
    • How do you trade relevance against diversity and novelty on one page of widgets?
    • How do you avoid a feedback loop that only recommends already-popular products?
  2. Design an agentic recommendation system for Amazon, covering component architecture, model choices, and quality and safety evaluation. [Reported] [6]
    • Which decisions should the LLM agent make, and which belong to a classical ranker?
    • How do you evaluate multi-turn recommendation quality before an A/B test?
    • How do you stop the agent from recommending unsafe, unavailable or irrelevant products?
  3. Design a recommender system (reported as the design round in an AS loop). [Reported] [15]
    • Two-tower retrieval, matrix factorization, or sequence models: which, and why?
    • How do you handle cold start for new users and new ASINs?
  4. A user goes from viewing to clicking to purchasing a product. Model the probability of each action given a user and a product. [Reported] [11]
    • Would you build three independent models, or model view, click and purchase jointly? [Reported] [11]
    • How would you build the dataset, choose features, and down-sample (for example with reservoir sampling)? [Reported] [11]
    • How do you handle the selection bias that purchases are only observed after clicks?
  5. Design "Frequently bought together" / "Customers who bought this also bought". [Likely]
    • How do you separate substitutes from complements?
    • How do you deal with spurious co-purchases from promotions or bundles?
  6. Design recommendations for Prime Video or Amazon Music, where consumption is sequential and the feedback is implicit. [Likely]
    • How do you define a positive label for a show that was watched for only 3 minutes?
  7. How would you personalize email or push notifications without fatiguing customers? [Likely]
    • How would you frame this as uplift modeling rather than response modeling?
  8. Design a size and fit recommendation system for apparel and shoes, so customers order the right size the first time. [Likely]
    • How do you learn from return reasons such as "too small" or "too large" as weak labels?
    • How do you handle brands whose sizing differs from the size printed on the label?
  9. A product is out of stock. Design a system that recommends substitutes on the detail page and in the cart. [Likely]
    • How do you tell a true substitute from a merely similar item, and how do you evaluate substitutability offline?
  10. How would you detect and fix popularity bias or exposure unfairness toward small sellers in recommendations? [Likely]

C. Product search and ranking

  1. You have a large collection of user click data for many search queries and product documents. Is click data alone sufficient? How would you train a retrieval or IR model with the data at hand? [Reported] [12]
    • How do you correct for position bias in clicks? [Likely] (see [23])
    • What other biases (presentation, trust, query intent) contaminate click labels?
  2. Walk through how you would design, train, validate and deploy a ranking model for product search. [Reported] [13]
    • How would you estimate true relevance from clickstream logs with position bias? [Reported] [13]
    • Pointwise, pairwise or listwise learning-to-rank: which would you choose, and why?
    • How do you define "relevant" for a query like "running shoes" versus "nike pegasus 40 size 10"? (search-relevance application questions are reported in [3])
  3. Compare bi-encoders and cross-encoders for retrieval and re-ranking. Where would each sit in a product-search stack? [Reported] [3]
    • What is your latency budget per stage, and how do you distill the cross-encoder?
  4. Design query understanding for Amazon search (intent, product type and attribute extraction). [Likely] (see [27])
    • How would you launch it in a new marketplace and language with little labeled data?
  5. Design a system to detect and reduce zero-result or low-quality-result searches. [Likely]
    • When should you relax the query, and when should you show a "did you mean"?
  6. How would you combine relevance with business objectives (conversion, margin, sponsored placement) in the final ranking? [Likely]
    • How do you stop business boosts from eroding relevance over time?
  7. Design an evaluation framework for search relevance using human annotation and LLM judges. [Likely]
    • How do you measure annotator agreement and calibrate an LLM judge against humans?
  8. How would you build semantic or vector search over a catalog of hundreds of millions of products? [Likely]
    • How do you handle index freshness for new listings and price or stock changes?

D. Advertising (Sponsored Products, DSP, Ads Trust)

  1. Design a system that recommends trustworthy ads to Amazon users while maintaining brand safety and compliance. [Reported] [9]
    • Walk through the full data pipeline from collection to deployment. [Reported] [9]
    • How do you trade ad revenue against trust and safety errors?
    • How do you get labels for rare policy violations?
  2. Design click-through-rate prediction for Sponsored Products ads. [Likely] (see [20])
    • Why does calibration matter in a second-price or first-price auction?
    • How do you handle delayed conversions in conversion-rate modeling?
  3. Revenue is flat, cost-per-click is up and conversions are down after a budget-pacing change. How would you diagnose it? [Likely]
  4. Design an ad-relevance or ad-moderation classifier for images and text in sponsored creatives. [Likely] (see [9])
    • How do you handle adversarial advertisers who iterate on rejected creatives?
  5. How would you measure the incremental impact (lift) of an ad campaign for an advertiser? [Likely]
    • Ghost ads, geo experiments or observational methods: what are the trade-offs?
  6. Design a bidding or budget-pacing algorithm for advertisers on Amazon DSP. [Likely]
    • How would you frame it as constrained optimization, or as a bandit problem?
  7. Design keyword or audience targeting recommendations for advertisers. [Likely]
  8. Design the ad-load and ad-placement policy for streaming video ads (Prime Video). How do you trade ad revenue against viewer engagement and churn? [Likely]
    • Which long-term metric would you use as a guardrail, and how do you measure it within an experiment's duration?

E. Fraud, abuse, trust and payments

  1. Design a fraud detection system for Amazon payments transactions. [Likely] (see [28])
    • How do you deal with extreme class imbalance and delayed chargeback labels?
    • How do you pick the operating threshold given the costs of false positives and false negatives?
    • How do you handle adversaries adapting to your model?
  2. How would you detect fake reviews or abusive sellers with heavily imbalanced and adversarial labels? [Likely]
    • Would you use graph-based signals across accounts, devices and payment instruments?
    • How do you evaluate when your labels come from your own past enforcement?
  3. Design a system to detect counterfeit products or listings. [Likely]
    • How do you combine image, text, seller history and brand-registry signals?
  4. Design account-takeover detection at login time under a strict latency budget. [Likely]
    • What do you do with the "uncertain" band: friction, step-up auth, or human review?
  5. Predict, at checkout time, the probability that an order will be returned. How would this be used, and what are the risks? [Likely]
    • How do you keep the model from penalizing protected groups or legitimate high-return categories?
  6. Design returns-abuse detection. How do you avoid punishing legitimate customers? [Likely]
  7. How would you prioritize a human-investigator queue when you can review only 1% of flagged cases? [Likely]
    • How do the investigators' decisions feed back into training without biasing it?
  8. Your fraud model's precision drops sharply over two weeks. Walk me through the investigation. [Likely]

F. Computer vision and catalog quality

  1. Use computer vision to detect defective products in fulfillment-center warehouses. [Reported] [8]
    • How do you collect and label defect images when defects are rare?
    • Edge or cloud inference, and what is the throughput requirement per station?
  2. You have 10k product listings across 100 categories, each with a title, description and photo. How would you categorize them? [Reported] [12]
    • How do you fuse text and image signals?
    • How do you handle a long tail of categories with very few examples?
    • How do you evaluate when the taxonomy itself is noisy?
  3. How would you detect and fix wrong attributes (size, color, brand) on listings at catalog scale? [Likely]
    • How would you use an LLM for attribute extraction, and how do you verify its outputs?
  4. Design duplicate-listing detection (the same product listed under multiple ASINs). [Likely]
    • How do you scale pairwise matching to hundreds of millions of items?

G. Forecasting, supply chain and inventory (SCOT)

  1. How would you forecast demand for millions of products, including new ones with no history? [Likely] (see [24][25][29])
    • Why use probabilistic or quantile forecasts rather than point forecasts for buying decisions?
    • Would you use a global model or per-item models, and why?
  2. What is the newsvendor problem, and how would you use it to set inventory levels? (Amazon supply chain interviews) [Reported] [26]
    • How does a quantile forecast map to the newsvendor critical ratio?
  3. How would you model an optimization problem from your work, including objective, decision variables and constraints? [Reported] [16]
    • When would you use a heuristic instead of an exact solver?
  4. Design an inventory placement system that decides which fulfillment centers should stock each product. [Likely] (see [25])
    • How do forecast errors propagate into placement and transportation costs?
  5. How would you forecast demand during Prime Day or other promotions, when history is sparse and distorted? [Likely]
    • How do you correct for censored demand when items were out of stock?
  6. How do you evaluate a forecasting system against a business outcome (in-stock rate, holding cost) rather than MAPE alone? [Likely]
    • How would you run an experiment on a buying policy when products share warehouses and trucks?
  7. Design a system to predict delivery time (the promise date shown on the detail page). [Likely] (see [19])
    • What loss would you use if a late delivery costs more than an early one?
    • How do you calibrate promises across regions and carriers?
  8. How would you improve address geocoding or delivery-location accuracy for last-mile delivery? [Likely] (see [19])
  9. Design a capacity or labor-planning forecast for fulfillment centers. [Likely]
  10. For grocery delivery (Amazon Fresh or Whole Foods), design the system that picks a replacement when an ordered item is unavailable at picking time. [Likely]
    • How do you learn each customer's tolerance for substitutions, and what is the success metric?

H. Pricing, causal inference and experimentation

  1. Design a system that recommends competitive prices for millions of products. [Likely] (see [29])
    • How do you estimate price elasticity when prices are set by your own algorithm (endogeneity)?
    • What guardrails stop the system from causing price spirals or customer-trust problems?
  2. How would you design an experiment to measure the effect of a pricing algorithm when prices interact across products? [Likely]
    • How do you handle interference between treated and control products, for example with switchback or cluster designs?
  3. How would you estimate the causal effect of a promotion without a clean experiment? [Likely]
    • When would you use double ML, instrumental variables, or difference-in-differences?
  4. How would you design an A/B test for a model launch? [Reported] [4]
    • What do you do if the test is underpowered or the metric is heavy-tailed?
    • How would you use variance reduction such as CUPED?
  5. A new ranking model wins offline but is neutral online. What could explain it, and what do you do next? [Likely]
  6. Design the system that chooses which seller's offer wins the featured offer (Buy Box) on a detail page. [Likely]
    • Which signals (price, delivery speed, seller performance) do you use, and how do you avoid being gamed by sellers?
    • How do you evaluate the effect on customers versus on seller fairness?
  7. How would you decide whether to discount a product, and by how much, for a deal event? [Likely]
    • How do you account for cannibalization and pull-forward demand?

I. Alexa, Rufus and LLM-powered products

  1. How would you improve Alexa speech recognition for non-native speakers and users with strong regional accents? [Reported] [10]
    • Where would you get diverse accent training data, and how would you handle phonetic variation? [Reported] [10]
    • How would you measure improvement per accent group without regressing the majority?
  2. Design an enterprise RAG (retrieval-augmented generation) service. [Reported] [14]
    • How do you chunk, embed and index heterogeneous documents?
    • How do you enforce document-level access control in retrieval?
    • How do you evaluate retrieval quality separately from generation quality?
  3. Evaluate an LLM agent with metrics beyond accuracy, such as tool-use correctness, hallucination and calibration. [Reported] [3]
    • How do you build an offline evaluation set for multi-step agent tasks?
    • When would you trust an LLM-as-judge?
  4. Design a GitHub Copilot-style coding agent (reported in a Senior AS loop, Boston). [Reported] [3]
    • What context does the agent retrieve, and how do you rank it?
    • How do you measure whether it helps developers (acceptance rate versus downstream bugs)?
  5. On an LLM team, discuss LLM decoding and training choices in the context of an Amazon use case. [Reported] [22]
    • Greedy, beam or sampling decoding for a customer-facing assistant: which would you use, and why?
  6. Design Rufus: answer "What do I need for cold weather golf?" using the catalog, reviews and Q&A. [Likely] (see [27][31][32])
    • How do you ground answers in product facts and avoid hallucinated specs?
    • How do you evaluate it offline before an A/B test, and what is the online success metric?
    • How do you mix organic and sponsored products in a generative answer?
  7. How would you summarize thousands of customer reviews into a short "Customers say" blurb? [Likely]
    • How do you measure faithfulness and coverage of minority opinions?
    • How do you stop manipulated reviews from steering the summary?
  8. Design an Alexa skill or intent router that maps an utterance to the right skill or API (natural-language understanding). [Likely]
    • How do you handle new skills with no training data?
    • What is your latency budget end to end, from wake word to response?
  9. How would you serve an LLM-based assistant to millions of users at acceptable cost and latency? [Likely]
    • Would you use distillation, quantization, caching, speculative decoding, or routing between small and large models?
  10. How would you fine-tune and align an assistant for a new language or marketplace (SFT, DPO, RLHF or RLAIF)? [Likely] (see [27])
    • How do you get preference data, and how do you detect reward hacking?
  11. How would you make an on-device model 10x smaller with minimal quality loss (Echo or Kindle devices)? [Likely]
  12. How would you detect and mitigate unsafe, biased or policy-violating responses from a shopping assistant? [Likely]
    • Which red-teaming process and which guardrail models would you use?
  13. Design proactive suggestions for Alexa ("hunches" or follow-up prompts) that are helpful without being intrusive. [Likely]
    • How do you set the interruption threshold, and how do you measure negative feedback that customers never state explicitly?
  14. Design a conversational customer-service agent that can issue refunds or replacements. [Likely]
    • Which actions require human-in-the-loop, and how do you decide?
    • How do you evaluate resolution rate without incentivizing over-refunding?

J. Scaling, MLOps and lifecycle (probes across any design)

  1. How do you monitor this model in production, and how do you detect data or concept drift? [Likely]
    • What triggers a retrain, and how do you validate it before rollout?
  2. How would you run inference at scale, in real time versus batch, and on what infrastructure (for example, SageMaker endpoints)? [Likely]
  3. How do you handle feature-store consistency and training/serving skew? [Likely]
  4. How would you roll back safely if the new model misbehaves on a subset of traffic? [Likely]
  5. Your labels arrive weeks late (returns, chargebacks, long-term retention). How do you train and evaluate under label delay? [Likely]
  6. How would you reduce the cost of this system by 50% with minimal quality loss? [Likely] (Frugality)
  7. Which parts of your design would you build, and which would you reuse from existing Amazon services or other teams? [Likely]
  8. How would you handle personally identifiable information and privacy constraints in the training data? [Likely]

K. L5-bar probes and LP crossovers inside the design round

  1. Of everything you proposed, what is the single riskiest assumption, and how would you de-risk it in two weeks? [Likely]
  2. A product manager wants to launch in a month, and your offline evaluation is incomplete. What do you do? [Likely] (Bias for Action versus Insist on the Highest Standards)
  3. Your design improves conversion but increases returns. How do you decide whether to ship it? [Likely]
  4. Which metric would you refuse to optimize directly, and why? [Likely]
  5. How would you explain the trade-offs in your design to a non-scientist stakeholder? [Likely] (Earn Trust)
  6. If you had 10x the data, or one-tenth of it, how would the design change? [Likely]
  7. How would you hand this system over to an engineering team for long-term ownership? [Likely]

L. Team-specific "warm-up" prompts to rehearse (inferred from postings and Amazon Science)

  1. (SCOT Buying Science) Design a system that decides how much of each product to buy from which vendor and when. [Likely] (see [29])
  2. (Payments) Design a model to predict payment-method failure and pick the best fallback for the customer. [Likely] (see [28])
  3. (Last mile) Design a model that predicts whether a package will be delivered successfully on the first attempt. [Likely] (see [19])
  4. (Alexa International) Design a multilingual natural-language-understanding model for a marketplace with code-switching (for example Hinglish). [Likely] (see [10][17])
  5. (Ads Trust) Design a policy-violation detection system for ads with an annotation budget of 5,000 labels per month. [Likely] (see [9])

07. LLM and GenAI Questions: Transformers, Fine-Tuning, RLHF/DPO, RAG, Evaluation, Hallucination, Efficiency, and Amazon Product Context (Bedrock, Nova, Rufus, Alexa+)

Scope: the LLM and GenAI questions an L5 Applied Scientist (Applied Scientist II) candidate should expect across the 2025-2026 onsite loop. This chapter is questions only, with no answers.

Research note (please read): this chapter draws on three kinds of evidence:

  • Candidate-report findings gathered for chapters 01-06 of this guide. They came from search-result extracts of the cited pages, not full reads. Each [Reported] tag cites the original page.
  • GitHub-hosted interview aggregators, fetched and read directly [22][23][24][25][26][35][36]. They compile Amazon candidate reports. Most cite trueinterview.io (CC BY 4.0); some do not name their sources. They are labeled as aggregators wherever used.
  • Amazon job postings and product pages, used only to ground [Likely] questions.

Verification status (October 2026 review pass). The shared web-search budget was used up, and the egress proxy refused connections (HTTP 403) to every non-GitHub host this pass tried: Glassdoor, Blind, LeetCode, 1point3acres, Medium, amazon.jobs, amazon.science, aboutamazon.com, IEEE Spectrum, IGotAnOffer, Dataford, Interview Kickstart, Enginebogie and OA VO Service.

  • Re-fetched and checked line by line: the seven GitHub aggregator pages [22][23][24][25][26][35][36].
  • Tags fixed after that check: questions whose tags those pages did not support were retagged [Likely] or relabeled. That covers transformers replacing RNNs, mixture-of-experts specifics, pretraining vs fine-tuning, LLM production trade-offs, the content-moderation attribution, and several round labels.
  • Not re-checked: the non-GitHub references [1]-[21] and [27]-[34] could not be re-opened. They are kept as captured from earlier search extracts, not confirmed dead. Re-verify them before relying on any single item.

Format notes for this round

  • There is usually no separate "LLM round." LLM and GenAI questions come up inside the standard loop seats: ML/Science Breadth, Science Depth (your own project), Science Application / ML System Design, and sometimes ML Coding.
    • Amazon's official page describes a loop of about four 55-minute interviews with members of the science community. Each interviewer is assigned technical competencies and Leadership Principles and typically asks 2-3 behavioral questions [1].
    • Candidate reports for AS II more often describe five rounds of 55-60 minutes, for example "1 Science ML Breadth, 1 Science Depth, 1 Sys Design (Team-specific), 1 coding round and 1 Bar Raiser" [2].
    • An aggregator built from about 20 Amazon science reports (mostly 2026) says generative-AI teams ask about LoRA, RLHF, GRPO, KV caching and distributed training. It also describes an optional online assessment covering PEFT, vLLM serving and image-text embeddings [22].
    • On generative-AI teams, "LLM depth is the most common technical reason for rejection" [23].
  • Transformers are now baseline breadth, even off-LLM teams. An L5 virtual-onsite thread on Blind treats transformers as core breadth [13]. An Amazon Ads candidate reported being rejected after not preparing for attention, transformers and LLMs [14]. 2025-2026 reports cover:
    • decoder-only inference and KV caching;
    • Longformer-style long-context attention;
    • bi-encoders vs cross-encoders;
    • LoRA;
    • BERT vs GPT;
    • DPO vs RLHF;
    • beam search;
    • "modern LLM training techniques" [3][4][7][8][10][11].
  • Team-dependent depth.
    • AGI/Nova-team loops are reported to lean hard on modern LLM training and ML coding. One aggregator reports a "debug a broken Transformer implementation" coding problem, and candidates compare the ML-coding style to OpenAI's [10][15][17][25]. Another aggregator says AGI candidate reports cover training techniques, evaluation (including perplexity and preference-based evals) and data quality [26].
    • LLM-team and Rufus/Alexa loops tie decoding, training and evaluation to an Amazon use case [8][27][28].
    • Science-application prompts in 2025-2026 include agentic products, agentic recommenders, enterprise RAG, LLM-agent evaluation and LLM quality validation [2][3][16][21][22].
  • Interviewer type. Usually Applied Scientists, Senior Applied Scientists or Principal Applied Scientists. The application round is often run by the hiring manager [1][2].
  • The L5 bar. L5 answers are expected to hold up through two or three layers of follow-up, against one layer at L4 [19]. Blind commenters say L5 candidates are vetted harder on whether they "raise the bar" [34]. For LLM topics that means:
    • deriving the mechanics (attention, the DPO loss, KV-cache memory);
    • naming failure modes and trade-offs;
    • choosing between prompting, RAG, fine-tuning and preference tuning for a concrete Amazon problem;
    • defining how you would evaluate it offline and online, including the cost and latency budget.
  • Leadership Principles still apply. Expect 1-2 LP questions inside each technical round [1][19]. A GenAI-flavored behavioral prompt is now reported in Amazon screens: how you use AI tools, and a time one gave you a wrong answer [24].

Tag legend.

  • [Reported] means a cited source attributes the question, or a close paraphrase, to an Amazon science interview. That is Applied Scientist unless noted. Items from the Research Scientist or MLE aggregators are marked "(Amazon RS aggregate)" or "(Amazon MLE aggregate)".
  • [Likely] means a grounded inference from the role, Amazon's products or job postings, or adjacent reported questions. It has not been seen verbatim.

A. Transformer fundamentals

A1. Architecture and attention

  1. Explain the Transformer architecture in detail: encoder, decoder, and what each component does. [Reported] [4][9][11]
    • Why scale the dot product by sqrt(d_k)? What happens to the softmax if you don't?
    • Why multiple heads instead of one wide head?
    • Where are the residual connections and layer norms, and why does pre-norm vs post-norm matter for training stability?
  2. Explain the attention mechanism "at root level," including scaled dot-product attention, multi-head attention and positional encoding. [Reported] [5][7]
    • Write the attention equation with shapes for Q, K and V.
    • How does the causal mask enter the equation?
  3. What happens if you set Wq = Wk = Wv in a Transformer? [Reported] [6]
    • What does that do to the attention matrix's symmetry, and what expressiveness do you lose?
  4. Explain the difference between LSTMs and Transformers mathematically. [Reported] [12]
    • How does each handle long-range dependencies, and what are the parallelism and compute trade-offs?
    • Why did transformers replace RNNs for language modeling? [Likely] (an Amazon-focused question list that says it is synthesized, not reported [26])
  5. Why do Transformers need positional information? Compare sinusoidal, learned, RoPE and ALiBi encodings. [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate: RoPE and ALiBi in a generative-AI phone screen) [22]
    • Write the RoPE formula and show how it makes attention depend only on relative position. [Reported] (Amazon RS and MLE aggregates) [22][23]
    • How does RoPE behave when you extrapolate past the training context length? [Likely]
  6. Compare multi-head and grouped-query attention, and extend the comparison to multi-query attention. [Reported] (Amazon MLE aggregate, Ads-team phone screen) [23]
    • How does each change KV-cache memory and quality?
  7. Transformer internals: how does the feed-forward block contribute compared with attention, and where do most of the parameters and FLOPs go? [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate, generative-AI breadth hour) [22]
    • Where do the parameter count and FLOPs come from as d_model, the number of layers and the sequence length change?
  8. Contrast Adam and AdamW. Why does AdamW decouple weight decay, and why does that matter for training transformers? [Reported] (Amazon RS and MLE aggregates) [22][23]
  9. You are handed a Transformer implementation that trains but performs badly. Debug it. [Reported] (Amazon AGI, aggregator) [25]
    • What bugs would you check for first (mask direction, softmax axis, scaling, positional-encoding offsets, layer-norm placement)?
  10. What is latent attention (low-rank compression of keys and values), and how does it cut KV-cache memory compared with GQA? [Reported] (Amazon MLE aggregate: "latent attention" listed among the most specialized training-at-scale screens) [23]
    • What does it cost in compute or quality, and how does it interact with RoPE? [Likely]

A2. Model families

  1. How do BERT and GPT differ in objectives, architecture and use cases? [Reported] [7]
    • How is BERT trained (MLM and NSP), and why did RoBERTa drop NSP?
    • When would an encoder-only model still beat a decoder-only LLM for an Amazon task, such as product classification?
  2. What is an encoder-decoder model such as T5, and how would you optimize it for inference? [Reported] [5]
  3. Compare LLM architectures: encoder-only vs decoder-only vs encoder-decoder, and dense vs mixture-of-experts. [Reported for the architecture discussion] (Amazon MLE aggregate: a rejected candidate described "a full round on architectures, scaling laws and RLHF trade-offs") [23]; MoE specifics [Likely]
    • How do you load-balance experts in MoE, and what goes wrong without it? [Likely]
  4. What do scaling laws say about how to split a fixed compute budget between parameters and tokens? [Reported] (Amazon MLE aggregate, LLM depth round) [23]
    • How would you use a scaling-law fit to decide whether to train a larger Nova-sized model or serve a smaller one? [Likely]
  5. Compare a small and a large model from the same text-embedding family. What do you gain, and what do you pay? [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate, breadth: a weak answer here was felt to hurt the round) [22]
    • Trace the history of embedding models (word2vec, then BERT pooling, then contrastive sentence encoders, then LLM-based embedders). What changed at each step? [Likely]

A3. LLM components written by hand

  1. Hand-write sigmoid, softmax and scaled dot-product attention, numerically stable, in NumPy or PyTorch. [Reported] (Amazon RS and MLE aggregates; sighting dated July 2026 in the Amazon question list) [22][23][36]
    • Where do you subtract the max, and how do you apply a causal mask without producing NaNs?

B. Inference, decoding and long context

  1. How does inference work for decoder-only models? [Reported] [3]
    • Walk through prefill vs decode. Which phase is compute-bound and which is memory-bandwidth-bound?
  2. What is the KV cache, and how does it speed up autoregressive generation? [Reported] [3][22][23]
    • Estimate the KV-cache memory for a given model (layers, heads, head dim, sequence length, batch). [Reported] (Amazon MLE aggregate: "memory arithmetic behind LoRA, quantization and KV caching") [23]
    • How do GQA/MQA, paged attention and cache quantization reduce it? [Likely]
  3. What makes standard self-attention expensive for long contexts? [Reported] [3]
    • What are the time and memory complexity in sequence length, and what does FlashAttention change (and not change)? [Reported] (Amazon MLE aggregate: flash attention in the depth round) [23]
  4. Explain Longformer and how its attention scales to long sequences. [Reported] [3]
    • Compare full attention, sparse or local attention, and other long-context approaches. [Reported] [3]
    • For a multi-turn shopping or search agent, when would you summarize or retrieve rather than extend the context window? [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate: multi-turn search agent with long-context handling) [22]
  5. How does beam search decoding work for LLMs? [Reported] [8]
    • When does beam search hurt open-ended generation, and why?
    • Compare greedy, beam, temperature, top-k and top-p sampling for a customer-facing assistant. [Reported] (LLM team: decoding choices for an Amazon use case) [8]
  6. How would you add constrained or structured decoding so an assistant always returns valid JSON for a tool call? [Likely]
    • What does it cost in latency and quality?
  7. What is speculative decoding, and when does it give a real speed-up? [Likely]
    • How do you pick the draft model, and how does the acceptance rate depend on the domain?
  8. How does prefix (prompt) caching relate to the KV cache? When does it cut cost and latency for a Bedrock-style workload? [Likely] (follow-up in an Amazon-focused synthesized list [26])
    • What cross-tenant leakage risk does a shared prompt cache create, and how do you prevent it?
  9. When is extra test-time compute (reasoning traces, self-consistency, best-of-n with a verifier) worth its latency and cost for a shopping or voice assistant? [Likely]
    • How would you decide per request whether to "think longer"?
  10. How do you evaluate whether a model actually uses a long context, beyond needle-in-a-haystack tests? [Likely]
    • How would you test for "lost in the middle" effects on real multi-document tasks?

C. Pretraining, fine-tuning and PEFT

  1. Which modern LLM training techniques do you know (pretraining, SFT, preference optimization)? [Reported] [10]
    • For an AGI-team role, expect several follow-ups in a row on each stage. [Reported] [10]
  2. Pretraining vs fine-tuning: what changes in data, objective and learning rate? [Likely]
    • What is catastrophic forgetting, and how do you detect and limit it?
  3. Walk me through an SFT training pipeline and its loss. [Reported] [19]
    • Why mask the prompt tokens in the loss?
    • How do you build and clean an instruction dataset for a domain like shopping or Alexa intents?
    • How do you mix general and domain data, and how would you choose the ratio?
  4. Explain the main parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques, which parameters each trains, and when each beats full fine-tuning. [Reported] (Amazon RS and MLE aggregates, online assessment) [22][23]; LoRA specifically [Reported] [4]
    • Explain LoRA. Which matrices do you adapt, and how do you choose the rank and alpha?
    • What is the memory cost of an adapter compared with full fine-tuning, counting optimizer states? [Reported] (Amazon MLE aggregate) [23]
    • Compare LoRA variants (QLoRA, DoRA, adapters, prefix tuning). [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate) [22]
  5. You need a domain-specialized model with limited compute. Do you use prompting, RAG, LoRA, full fine-tuning or continued pretraining? [Likely]
    • What evidence would make you move from one option to the next?
    • How does the answer change if the base model is only reachable as a hosted endpoint, for example through Bedrock? [Likely]
  6. If you used knowledge distillation from an LLM, what exactly was distilled, and why? [Reported] [20]
    • Why distill instead of quantizing or pruning?
    • How would you distill an LLM into a small classifier using unlabeled data? [Likely] (see Amazon Science work on LLM distillation [31])
  7. How would you generate synthetic training data with an LLM without collapsing diversity or leaking evaluation data? [Likely]
  8. Your fine-tuning run's loss spikes at step 40k. How do you debug it? [Likely]
    • Which checks would you run on the data, the learning-rate schedule, mixed precision and gradient norms?
  9. How does tokenizer choice (BPE vocabulary size, byte fallback) affect cost, latency and quality across Alexa's locales and scripts? [Likely] [27][32]
    • How would you extend a tokenizer for a new language without retraining from scratch?

D. Alignment: RLHF, DPO and beyond

  1. What is the difference between DPO and RLHF? [Reported] [7]
    • Derive how DPO removes the explicit reward model. What role does the reference model play?
    • What does DPO's beta control, and what happens at very small or very large beta?
  2. Explain RLHF and PPO, including the clipped objective. [Reported] (Amazon RS and MLE aggregates) [22][23]
    • Why include a KL penalty to the SFT policy, and what failure mode does it prevent?
  3. Compare PPO, GRPO and GSPO. Why does GRPO drop the critic? [Reported] (Amazon RS and MLE aggregates) [22][23]
    • What does removing the critic cost, and how would you parallelize GRPO training? [Reported] (generative-AI phone screen; specialized screens) [22][23]
    • When would you prefer verifiable rewards (RLVR) over a learned reward model? [Likely]
  4. If you have run GRPO-style training yourself: which instabilities did you hit (entropy collapse, reward spikes, length blow-up), and how did you fix them? [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate: post-training teams look for "evidence you have run GRPO-style training and fixed its instabilities") [22]
  5. RLHF in practice: what goes wrong at scale? [Reported] (Amazon MLE aggregate: "RLHF in practice" and "RLHF trade-offs" in depth rounds) [23]
    • Walk through reward hacking, length bias, sycophancy and mode collapse. [Likely]
    • How would you detect reward hacking from logs? [Likely]
  6. How do you design a reward signal or preference dataset for a shopping assistant? [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate: "how would you design the reward") [22]
    • Who labels, how do you measure agreement, and how do you handle annotator disagreement?
    • Where could RLAIF replace human labels, and where can it not? [Likely] (Alexa postings list SFT, DPO, RLHF and RLAIF [27])
  7. Your DPO-tuned model gives longer but less accurate answers. Diagnose it. [Likely]
    • How do you control for length in the preference data and in evaluation?
  8. How would you align a multilingual Alexa model when preference data is scarce in some locales? [Likely] (Alexa International Tech posting [27]; the Alexa International loop guide [32])

E. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)

  1. Design an enterprise RAG service. [Reported] [16]
    • How do you chunk, embed and index heterogeneous documents?
    • How do you enforce per-user, document-level access control at query time, when permissions change between crawls?
    • How do you evaluate retrieval quality separately from generation quality?
  2. What is the difference between a cross-encoder and a bi-encoder for retrieval? [Reported] [3]
    • When would you use each in a retrieve-then-rerank pipeline, and with what latency budget?
    • When would you distill a cross-encoder into a bi-encoder? [Likely] [31]
  3. Explain RAG, and where it helps and does not help with hallucination. [Reported] (Amazon RS aggregate: LoRA, RAG and LLM agents in breadth) [22]
    • What failure modes remain once the right passage is retrieved (ignoring it, misreading it, or mixing it with parametric knowledge)?
  4. Compare dense, sparse (BM25) and hybrid retrieval for product search queries. [Likely]
    • How do you handle exact attributes such as model numbers, sizes and brand names?
  5. Contrastive embedding training: write a numerically stable, symmetric InfoNCE loss for paired image and text embeddings. [Reported] (Amazon RS and MLE aggregates, coding round) [22][23]
    • How do hard negatives change what the encoder learns, and how do you mine them without false negatives?
  6. Rufus-style question: build retrieval-augmented answers to "What do I need for cold weather golf?" over the catalog, reviews and community Q&A. [Likely] [28][29][30]
    • How do you ground claims in product facts and avoid made-up specifications?
    • How do you handle stale or conflicting sources (listing vs reviews)?
    • How do you show citations, and how do you check that a citation supports the claim?
  7. How would you decide between a longer context window and better retrieval for an assistant? [Likely]
  8. How would you evaluate a RAG system offline (context recall and precision, faithfulness, answer relevance)? [Likely]
    • How do you build the golden set, and how often do you refresh it?
  9. Much of the catalog is structured (attributes, prices, availability). When would you answer through a structured lookup or text-to-query tool instead of text retrieval, and how would the LLM decide? [Likely]
  10. Use an LLM to rewrite or expand product-search queries. How do you keep it inside a search latency budget (offline caching for head queries, a distilled model for the tail)? How do you measure the effect on relevance? [Likely]
  11. Would generative retrieval or semantic IDs (an LLM that decodes item identifiers) work for Amazon-scale product recommendation? What breaks as the catalog changes daily? [Likely]

F. Agents and tool use

  1. Pick an agentic product we could build. Who is the customer, and how do you measure success? [Reported] [2]
