10. Bar Raiser Round: Reported Questions, Mixed Technical and Behavioral Probing, and Follow-up Patterns

Scope: the one interview in the L5 Applied Scientist (Applied Scientist II) onsite run by the Bar Raiser (BR). It covers who the BR is, what they assess at L5, the questions candidates report, the questions the format makes likely, and the follow-up patterns BRs use to test whether a story holds up.

Research note (please read): in this review pass, the web-search budget was already used up, and page fetches to the cited sites (4dayweek.io, igotanoffer.com, interviewkickstart.com, dataford.io, barraiserai.com, amazon.jobs) failed with DNS errors or proxy refusals. The one source family that could be read in full was the GitHub question-bank aggregator [28]-[32]. It was cloned and read directly; it says its content is based on candidate reports, mostly from Q1-Q3 2026. Every question tagged [Reported] that cites [28]-[32] was checked against that text. References [1]-[27] carry over from chapters 01-08 of this guide and were not re-fetched in this pass. Their [Reported] tags rest on search-result extracts gathered earlier, so treat them as unverified until someone re-opens the pages.

Format notes

Aspect What is known Sources
Length and slot One of the onsite loop interviews. Amazon's official AS page describes the loop as four 55-minute interviews with members of the science community. Candidates more often report 5 rounds of 55-60 minutes, one of them labeled "Bar Raiser". The BR round is sometimes held on a separate day, from the day before the loop to weeks after it. [1][8][9][13][28]
Who the BR is A trained interviewer from outside the hiring team. BRs are nominated, trained, and shadow veteran BRs before they run debriefs. Science candidates report BRs from non-tech orgs, and one AS II candidate was told the final interview would be a Principal TPM. [2][3][5][6][10][29]
Power The BR chairs the debrief and, with the hiring manager, makes the hire/no-hire call. The BR holds an effective veto that former Amazonians say is rarely used explicitly. [2][3][7][12][29]
What is assessed For scientists the round is usually entirely behavioral: 3-4 LP questions, each followed many layers down. Customer Obsession, Learn and Be Curious and Insist on the Highest Standards are the LPs named for science BRs. In one L6 loop the BR also asked for coding (a priority queue from scratch). The "better than 50% of current employees at that level" line is BR folklore, not a published Amazon standard. [4][5][6][28][29][30]
ML content in stories Science loops assign each round 2-3 LPs; one five-round loop covered ten different LPs. Interviewers expect stories to contain an ML decision about models, data or experiments, and one team required ML work in every story. "A disagreement about an evaluation metric beats one about meeting schedules." [28][29][30]
L5-specific bar L5 means owning a project end to end, making the technical calls, and influencing peers and sometimes sister teams. A vendor-published debrief says L5 LP answers face 2-3 layers of follow-up, against 1 layer at L4. Blind commenters say LP narratives, not coding, separate L5 from L6. Scope is judged against the target level, so solid work can still be marked down for insufficient impact. [14][15][18][29][30]
Leveling A BR can push the level down even when the HM is positive. One L5 candidate's BR saw them "at the cusp of L4 and L5". Downleveling from L6 to L5 and from L5 to L4 is commonly reported. [11][15]
Behavioral weight overall Amazon says each interviewer typically asks 2-3 behavioral questions. One candidate counted 17 LP questions in a four-hour loop. In one five-round science loop, LP questions left only about 15 minutes for coding in the fourth hour. [1][16][18][28][29]
Split decisions and cooldowns When the debrief splits, candidates report an extra round, for example one more coding round for an AGI-team AS II. Recent science and ML rejections carried cooldowns of 6-12 months. [17][29][30]

Observed probing patterns (what makes the BR round different)

  • Layered "why" and "how do you know": 3-4 layers on one story: why that path, which alternatives, what data, what you would change. [18][19][21][22][28]
  • "I" not "we": BRs separate your individual contribution from the team's, down to dates, data and your exact role. [21][22][28][30]
  • Deliberate pushback: candidates report BRs saying the approach was wrong, or repeatedly saying "that is not what I'm looking for", to see whether the candidate defends with data or switches to a better story. [21][22][29][30]
  • After-commit focus: on disagree-and-commit stories, the grade turns on what you did after committing, for example quietly reducing the risk you had raised. [28][29][31]
  • Metric interrogation: "How was that measured?", including baselines, attribution and counterfactuals. [21][22]
  • Lay-audience translation: a non-science BR (for example a Principal TPM, or a backend engineer BR in another track) asks for project-management mechanics, basic stats interpretation, or technical trade-offs in plain language. [10][32]
  • Closing sweep: some BRs end with an open invitation to share anything earlier interviewers missed. [32]

Questions

Tag legend:

  • [Reported]: a candidate or source attributes this question, or a close paraphrase, to an Amazon BR round or to Amazon AS/science LP questioning. When the report comes from another round or track, the item says so in parentheses.
  • [Likely]: an inference grounded in the BR format, the 16 LPs, the L5 bar and Amazon's science products. It has not been seen verbatim.

Indented bullets are follow-up probes. In a BR round, expect at least two of them per story.

A. Opening and scope calibration (sets the level the BR will argue in the debrief)

  1. Walk me through your career and the one project that best shows the level you are operating at today. [Likely]
    • What part of that project would not have happened without you?
    • Who else could have done what you did on that project?
    • If I called your manager, what would they say was your scope?
  2. Tell me about the largest-scope science project you have owned end to end, from problem framing to production. [Likely]
    • Where did the problem come from: did you find it or were you handed it?
    • What was the decision you made that most changed the outcome?
    • How many people or teams depended on your output?
  3. Describe your exact individual contribution to that project, without saying "we". [Reported] [21][22][28]
    • Which design document or analysis did you write yourself?
    • What did you delegate, and why?
    • That sounds like a team result. What specifically did you do? [Reported] [21][22]
  4. Tell me about a time your work influenced a team other than your own. [Likely]
    • How did they adopt it: mechanism, code, paper or persuasion?
    • What would have happened if they had said no?
  5. What is the hardest technical problem you have solved in the last two years? [Likely]
    • Why was it hard for you specifically, and not just for the field?
    • Explain it so someone outside ML understands why it mattered.
  6. Give me your flagship project in two minutes. Now take ten minutes and go deeper on the part you are proudest of. [Likely] [29]
    • What did you leave out of the two-minute version that a skeptic would ask about?

B. Conflict, backbone and disagree-and-commit

  1. Tell me about a time you had a conflict with a coworker or manager, and how you handled it. [Reported] [19][21][22][29][30]
    • What did you do once the decision went against you?
    • Looking back, who was right?
    • How is your relationship with that person now?
  2. Tell me about a time you had a major conflict with a Principal Engineer (or a more senior scientist). How did you resolve it? [Reported] [24]
    • What data did you bring to the conversation?
    • Did you escalate? Why or why not?
  3. Tell me about a time a client or decision maker proposed a plan that was far too aggressive. What did you do? [Reported] [29][30]
    • What evidence showed you the plan was unrealistic?
    • What did you counter-propose, and what did they accept?
  4. Tell me about a time you disagreed with a product manager's launch decision based on your model's results. [Likely]
    • What metric were they looking at that you thought was wrong?
    • What happened after launch, and were you proven right?
    • What would have convinced you to commit to their plan?
  5. Tell me about a time you disagreed with a manager or challenged a team decision, committed anyway, and what happened next. [Reported] [28][30][31]
    • After you committed, how did you reduce the specific risk you had raised? [Reported] [28][31]
    • How did you make sure your doubts did not slow the team down?
    • Did you ever revisit the decision? What triggered that?
  6. Tell me about a disagreement with a colleague over which evaluation metric or baseline to use. [Likely] [29]
    • How did you settle it: argument, experiment or escalation?
    • Which metric does the team use today?
  7. Tell me about a time an engineer said your model could not ship in its current form. [Likely]
    • What did you give up to get it into production?
    • Who made the final call?
  8. Tell me about a time you had to tell a senior leader that their idea would not work scientifically. [Likely]
    • How did you frame it?
    • What did they do next?
  9. Describe a time you saw your team heading in the wrong direction. What did you do? [Reported] [32] (front-end BR round, same format)
    • How did you know before the results showed it?
    • Whose decision was it to change course?
  10. Tell me about a time you raised a risk, were overruled, and the risk then happened. [Likely]
    • What did you say afterwards, and to whom?

C. Failure, being wrong and learning

  1. Tell me about a project failure and how you managed the aftermath. [Reported] [24]
    • What would you do differently? [Reported] [20][28]
    • What mechanism did you put in place so it does not happen again?
  2. Tell me about a time your model underperformed in production and you had to diagnose the issue. [Reported] [23][30]
    • What did you learn, and how did you pivot? [Reported] [25]
    • How long did it take you to notice, and why that long?
  3. Tell me about a time you were wrong about a technical call. How did you find out? [Likely]
    • Who told you, or did you find it yourself?
    • What did it cost the team?
    • Did you have to admit it in front of the team or stakeholders?
  4. Tell me about a time you got critical feedback that surprised you. [Reported] [28] (listed as a recurring BR theme)
    • What did you change afterwards, and how would someone see that change?
  5. What do you most need to improve, and what have you changed so far? [Reported] [31] (hiring-manager round)
    • What evidence would your manager point to that you have improved?
  6. Tell me about an experiment or research direction you killed. [Likely]
    • How did you decide it was dead rather than under-tuned?
    • How much effort had gone in, and how did you tell the team?
  7. Describe a deadline you were going to miss: when you realized it, who you told, and what you proposed. [Reported] [28]
    • Why didn't you see it earlier?
  8. Tell me about a time AI gave you a wrong answer. How did you catch it? [Reported] [28][31]
    • What do you now check by hand that you used to trust?
    • How did you realize the output of a generative AI tool was not good enough, and what did you do? [Reported] [31] (hiring-manager round)
  9. How would you test code or analysis that an AI assistant generated for one of your experiments? [Reported] [31] (hiring-manager round; adapted from "AI-generated code")
    • What failure would slip past your unit tests but break the experiment?

D. Decisions under ambiguity (Bias for Action, Are Right a Lot)

  1. Describe a decision you made with incomplete information and how you later validated it. [Reported] [25][29]
    • What data would you have wanted, and why couldn't you wait for it?
    • How did you limit the downside if you were wrong?
    • Was it a one-way or two-way door? What would you have done if it had turned out wrong?
  2. Describe a calculated risk you took: what you knew at the time and how it turned out. [Reported] [28][29][30]
    • What was the worst case you planned for?
    • Who else knew about the risk before you took it?
  3. Tell me about a time you took initiative without waiting for permission. [Likely]
    • Whose permission would you normally have needed?
    • What did your manager say afterwards?
  4. Tell me about a time you had to choose between a quick heuristic and a principled model. [Likely]
    • What did you ship first, and did the principled model ever replace it?
  5. Tell me about a time two offline metrics disagreed and you had to make a ship call. [Likely]
    • Which metric did you trust, and why?
  6. Tell me about a time the online A/B result fell short of, or contradicted, your offline gain. How did you find out why? [Reported] [29][30] (science-depth and project-dive rounds)
    • What was the root cause?
    • Did you change your offline evaluation afterwards?
  7. Tell me about a time you settled a technical debate with a quick experiment instead of more argument. [Likely]
    • How did you make sure both sides would accept the result?

E. Ownership and Deliver Results (the core L5 signal)

  1. Tell me about a time you took ownership of something outside your formal scope. [Reported] [28] (listed as a recurring BR theme)
    • Why didn't the actual owner handle it?
    • What did you stop doing to make room?
  2. Tell me about a time you delivered a model under a hard deadline. [Likely]
    • What did you descope, and what did that cost?
    • What corners did you refuse to cut?
  3. Tell me about the most significant business result your science has driven. [Likely]
    • How was that measured? [Reported] [21][22]
    • How do you know it was the model and not seasonality, a UI change or a marketing push? What was the counterfactual?
    • What was the baseline?
  4. Tell me about a time a project was about to fail and you turned it around. [Likely]
    • What was the first thing you did differently?
  5. Tell me about a time you had to keep a model working long after launch (monitoring, retraining, on-call). [Likely]
    • Who owned it when it broke at 2am?
  6. Tell me about a time you were blocked by another team. [Likely]
    • What did you do in the first week?
    • Did you find a way around them, or did you wait?
  7. Tell me about a time two partner teams wanted your model to optimize different metrics. [Likely]
    • Whose metric won, and how was that decided?

F. Customer Obsession and Working Backwards, for a scientist

  1. Tell me about a time you went above and beyond for a customer. [Reported] [29][30]
    • Was the customer internal or external?
    • What did going beyond cost you or the team?
  2. Tell me about a time you started from the customer and worked backwards to the science problem. [Likely]
    • Who was the customer, and how did you get their signal?
  3. Tell me about a time you chose a simpler model because it was better for the customer. [Likely]
    • What accuracy did you give up, and how did you justify it?
  4. Tell me about a time a model you built hurt some customers even though the average metric improved. [Likely]
    • How did you find that segment?
    • What did you do about it?
  5. Tell me about a time you used customer anecdotes to overturn what the data said. [Likely]
    • How many anecdotes was enough?
  6. If you wrote a press release for your best project, what would the headline and customer quote be? [Likely]
    • What would the FAQ's hardest question be?

G. Dive Deep and scientific rigor (the technical side of the BR round)

  1. Describe a time you found an issue nobody else had noticed: how you isolated it, and why your first hypothesis was wrong. [Reported] [18][27][31][32]
    • What was the first anomaly you noticed?
    • How did you rule out the other explanations?
  2. Tell me about a time you found a data-quality or label-leakage problem in your own work. [Likely]
    • How long had it been in production?
    • Which numbers did you have to retract?
  3. Walk through a major technical decision from your resume, the alternatives you rejected, and whether you would make the same choice again. [Reported] [28][31]
    • What experiment told you the alternative was worse?
    • Who did you have to convince?
  4. For your main project or publication, what was your specific contribution, and how did you choose the baselines? [Reported] [29] (science-depth round)
    • Which strong baseline did you leave out, and why?
  5. Walk me through how you validated that your evaluation set reflected production traffic. [Likely]
    • What distribution shift did you see after launch?
  6. Tell me about a time a result looked too good to be true. [Likely]
    • What did you check first?
  7. Tell me about a time you had to defend a statistical result to a skeptical audience. [Likely]
    • What was the effect size, and was it practically significant?
  8. You mentioned an improvement of X%. What was the confidence interval? [Likely]
    • How many samples, and how long did the test run?
  9. Tell me about a time you reproduced a paper's results and could not match them. [Likely]
    • What did you conclude, and what did you ship instead?
  10. What is the biggest weakness of your flagship model that I have not asked about yet? [Likely] [29]
    • What would it take to fix it?

H. Technical checks from a non-science Bar Raiser (TPM, SDE or business BR)

  1. How do you set ETAs, map dependencies and communicate next steps on a science project? [Reported] [10]
    • When did a science timeline slip, and how did you tell stakeholders?
    • How do you estimate a project when you do not know if the idea will work?
  2. Interpret a p-value or a confidence interval for me. [Reported] [10]
    • What would you tell a business leader who reads p = 0.06 as "no effect"?
  3. Explain your most recent model to me as if I were a business leader with no ML background. [Likely]
    • What decision does it let someone make that they could not make before?
  4. Tell me about a technique you learned on your own and applied. How did it hold up across the conditions it had to handle? [Likely] [32] (asked in a front-end BR about a front-end technology across devices; adapted here to ML)
    • Explain the trade-off to me as a non-specialist.
  5. How do you decide when a research prototype is ready to hand to engineering? [Likely]
    • What does the handoff document contain?
  6. Your science project needs six months but the business wants results in six weeks. What do you do? [Likely]
    • What would a six-week version look like?
  7. How do you know a model is still working a year after launch? [Likely]
    • Who gets paged, and what is the rollback plan?
  8. Tell me about a time you had to explain a model's mistake to a customer-facing or operations team. [Likely]

I. Invent and Simplify, Think Big, Frugality, Learn and Be Curious

  1. Tell me about a time you simplified a complex scientific approach so it could ship. [Likely]
    • What did the complex version get right that the simple one missed?
  2. Tell me about the most ambitious science bet you have proposed. [Likely]
    • Who funded it, and what did you have to prove first?
    • If it had been fully funded, what would the three-year version be?
  3. Tell me about a time you did more with less: compute, labels or headcount. [Likely]
    • What was the cost of the cheaper approach in model quality?
  4. Tell me about something you invented, such as a method, tool or mechanism, that others now use. [Likely]
    • How did you get people to adopt it?
  5. Tell me about a time you used an LLM or foundation model where a custom model would have been the default. [Likely]
    • How did you weigh inference cost against development time?
  6. Tell me about a time curiosity about an odd pattern in data led you to a new direction for the project. [Likely] [29] (Learn and Be Curious, named for science BRs)
    • How much time did you spend before you told anyone?
  7. Tell me about a time you learned a new area fast to unblock a project. [Likely]
    • How did you know you had learned enough?

J. Earn Trust, Hire and Develop the Best, influence without authority

  1. Tell me about a time you earned the trust of an engineering or product partner who doubted your science. [Likely]
    • What was the turning point?
  2. Tell me about a time a teammate's work did not meet the bar, and what you did. [Reported] [29]
    • Did you fix it yourself or help them fix it? Why?
    • How did they react, and what changed afterwards?
  3. Tell me about a time you mentored someone or raised the bar of your team's science. [Likely]
    • How do you know they improved?
  4. Tell me about a time you influenced a decision without having authority over the people involved. [Likely]
    • Who was the hardest person to convince?
  5. Tell me about a time you reviewed someone else's experiment and found a serious flaw. [Likely]
    • How did you raise it?

K. Insist on the Highest Standards, Broad Responsibility

  1. Tell me about a time you raised the quality bar on a deliverable even though it delayed you. [Likely] [29]
    • Who disagreed with the delay?
  2. Tell me about a time you refused to launch something that was not ready. [Likely]
    • What was the minimum bar, and who set it?
  3. Tell me about a time you insisted on better labels or data quality when the team wanted to train on what they had. [Likely] [29]
    • How did you show the cost of the noisy data?
  4. Tell me about a time you considered the downstream harm or fairness impact of a model. [Likely]
    • How did you measure it?
    • What trade-off did you accept?
  5. Tell me about a time you handled sensitive customer data in a modeling project. [Likely]
    • What would you not do even if it improved the model?

L. Deliberate pushback and stress probes (BR-specific)

  1. I think your approach was wrong. Convince me otherwise. [Reported] [21][22]
    • What data would make you concede?
    • What would the alternative have cost?
  2. That is not what I'm looking for. Give me a different example for the same principle. [Reported] [29][30]
  3. That example is from three years ago. Do you have a more recent one? [Likely]
  4. Your manager made that call. Where was your judgment in that? [Likely]
  5. If you had not been on the project, what would have been different? [Likely]
  6. What was the worst decision you made on that project? [Likely]
  7. What would your harshest critic on that team say about how you handled it? [Likely]
  8. You said the result was a success. Who would disagree? [Likely]
  9. Stop the story there. What exactly did you say in that meeting? [Likely]

M. Leveling probes: L4 vs L5 vs L6

  1. Tell me about a time you set the technical direction for a project, not just executed it. [Likely]
    • Who was in the room when the direction was decided?
  2. Tell me about a time you identified a science problem nobody had asked you to solve. [Likely]
    • How did you get time and resources for it?
  3. Tell me about a mechanism (process, tool, review) you created that outlived your involvement. [Likely]
    • Who runs it now?
  4. Tell me about a time you had to coordinate science work across several people or teams. [Likely]
    • How did you divide the work, and what broke?
  5. What is the biggest ambiguity you have resolved, from vague goal to concrete plan? [Likely]
    • What was the first artifact you produced?
  6. How would your current manager describe the gap between you and the next level? [Likely]
    • What are you doing about it?
  7. What was the largest measured business effect of your work? [Likely] [29][30] (scope is judged against the target level)
    • If it were twice as big, what would you have done differently?

N. Mixed technical-behavioral prompts (BR who is a scientist)

  1. Walk through a ranking (or similar) project: the pain point, why this model, the metrics, and how you diagnosed a disappointing A/B result. [Reported] [30] (project deep-dive round)
    • What would you change in the experiment design today?
  2. For a model you built, why did you choose that backbone, how did you handle class imbalance, and what would you do with more resources? [Reported] [29][30] (science-depth and project-dive rounds)
    • Which of those choices would you defend to a skeptical Principal Scientist?
  3. Explain an AI-agent or LLM project you built and how it got adopted. [Reported] [30] (project deep-dive round)
    • Who used it in the first month, and who stopped using it?
  4. Tell me about a time a technical trade-off (latency, cost, accuracy) forced a business compromise. [Likely]
    • Who signed off on the compromise?
  5. Tell me about a time you moved a model from research to production at scale. [Likely]
    • What failed first in production that never failed offline?
  6. Tell me about a time you had to choose between publishing and shipping. [Likely]
    • What did the business get out of the choice?
  7. Tell me about a GenAI or LLM feature you built or evaluated. How did you decide it was safe to launch? [Likely]
    • How did you measure hallucination, and what threshold did you accept?
  8. Tell me about a time an evaluation metric you chose turned out to be the wrong proxy. [Likely]
    • How did you detect the mismatch?
  9. (Senior BR adding coding) Implement a priority queue from scratch. [Reported] [28] (reported in an L6 loop; possible but less likely at L5)
    • What is the cost of each operation, and how would you support decrease-key?

O. Post-loop, split-debrief and closing scenarios

  1. (Extra round after a split debrief) Let's revisit coding with a follow-up problem. [Reported] [17]
  2. (Downlevel conversation) We think your scope maps to L4. Would you consider that level? [Likely] (BR-driven downleveling reported in [11][15])
    • What evidence from your past work would change our assessment?
  3. Why Amazon, and why this team? [Reported] [28]
    • Which LP do you find hardest to live by?
  4. Is there anything the earlier interviewers did not ask that you want me to know? [Reported] [32]
    • Which LP does that story show?
  5. What questions do you have for me about how Amazon hires and develops scientists? [Likely]

11. Amazon Science Team Context: What Each Hiring Org Asks, and the Recent Amazon Science Work to Know

Scope: the team-specific part of the L5 Applied Scientist (Applied Scientist II) onsite. That means the hiring-manager / Science Application round, the "why this team" probes, and the "have you seen our work?" questions. It covers the orgs that most often hire L5 AS: Search (Stores Foundational AI, formerly M5), Ads, Alexa AI / AGI (Nova), AWS AI, Rufus, Personalization and recommendations, SCOT (supply chain), Pricing, and Prime Video. It also lists recent Amazon Science publications and open-source releases, framed as questions an interviewer might ask. This chapter is questions only, with no answers.

Research note (please read): in both the first pass and the October 2026 review pass, the shared web-search budget was used up and only github.com could be reached. Glassdoor, Blind, Medium, LeetCode, 1point3acres, amazon.jobs, amazon.science, aboutamazon.com, IEEE Spectrum and the prep-vendor sites did not resolve from this environment. The evidence comes from two places:

  • Candidate-report findings already gathered and cited in chapters 01-09 of this guide. Those were built from search-result extracts of the cited pages, not full reads. Each [Reported] tag here cites the original page, and I tag a question [Reported] only when one of those chapters attributes it to an Amazon science interview. These non-GitHub citations could not be re-fetched in the review pass, so treat them as unverified.
  • Pages fetched and read directly: the amazon-science GitHub organization and more than 20 of its repositories [33]-[49], [60]-[69], and four pages from two GitHub interview aggregators [19][20][21][59]. The review pass re-read the aggregators. As a result, it retagged two items that the aggregators list only as round topics, not as asked questions (RoPE/ALiBi/KV caching, and the fraud-metrics item), and it fixed the DeepMTL2R method count.

Team-specific first-person reports for Prime Video and Pricing were not found, so every question in those sections is [Likely]. Before your loop, ask your recruiter for the team's name and re-check its newest postings and papers. A follow-up session with a fresh search budget could add Blind and 1point3acres threads for each org.

Format notes for this round

  • Where team context shows up. Every L5 loop includes a team-tailored round, usually called "Science Application" or "ML System Design (team-specific)", and it is often run by the hiring manager or a senior scientist from the hiring team. One 2025-2026 AS II loop was reported as "1 Science ML Breadth, 1 Science Depth, 1 Sys Design (Team-specific), 1 coding round and 1 Bar Raiser" [2]. Amazon's own prep page describes about four 55-minute interviews with members of the science community. It says each interviewer is assigned competencies and asks 2-3 behavioral (Leadership Principle) questions [1]. Blind commenters say L5 loops usually include the application/design round and L4 loops may not [53].
  • Duration and interviewer. Rounds run 55-60 minutes. Expect about 40 minutes on the team problem and 10-15 minutes on LPs [1][52]. The HM also judges team fit and, with the bar raiser, makes the final call [53].
  • What the L5 bar looks like here. You take a vague problem from the team's domain and frame it end to end: data and labels, model, offline and online metrics, launch, and monitoring. You make the trade-off calls yourself [17][52]. Depth on the team's own stack, and awareness of what the team has published, separate a "strong hire" from a "hire" in this round. That point is an inference, not a published rubric.
  • The team can change after the loop. Some candidates pass the loop and are then team-matched or "recycled" to another org's opening [54]. Prepare a 2-minute pitch on how your work maps to at least two orgs.
  • GenAI everywhere. Even on non-LLM teams, 2025-2026 loops treat transformers and LLMs as core breadth. An Ads candidate reported being rejected after not preparing for them [5][50].
  • Org churn (treat as unverified). One GitHub aggregator reports that Amazon's AGI org was reorganized in 2025-2026 and that Nova 2 Lite, Nova 2 Sonic, Nova Forge and Nova Act remain active products [21]. An earlier chapter's search extract says Rufus was renamed "Alexa for Shopping" in May 2026 (see [27]). Confirm the current org and product names with your recruiter.

Tag legend: [Reported] means a cited source attributes the question, or a close paraphrase, to an Amazon science or ML interview. Items from aggregators are marked "(aggregator)", and items from Research Scientist or MLE loops are marked "(RS loop)" or "(MLE loop)". [Likely] means a grounded inference from the team's products, job postings or Amazon Science publications. It has not been seen verbatim.

A. Cross-team "why this team" and team-fit questions

  1. Why Amazon, why Applied Science, and why this particular team? [Likely]
    • Which of our team's products have you used as a customer, and what would you change first?
    • Which of our team's papers or launches have you read?
  2. How would you apply your research or project to a problem at Amazon? [Reported] [55]
    • Which team's metric would your work move, and by how much, roughly?
  3. (Recruiter or HM screen for a GenAI team.) What hands-on experience do you have with LLM post-training or with building agents? [Reported] (aggregator) [59]
    • Which part did you personally own: data, reward design, training infrastructure or evaluation?
  4. Here is a high-level ML problem from our team. Propose data sources, an annotation strategy, a modeling technique, evaluation metrics, and the trade-offs and risks. [Reported] [17]
    • What is the version you would ship in one quarter, and what would v2 add?
  5. Answer an ML application question about one of our Amazon products. [Reported] [18]
  6. Pick an agentic product we could build. Who is the customer, and how do you measure success? [Reported] (Seattle, 2026) [2]
    • Write the press-release headline (Working Backwards).
  7. If the loop goes well but this team's headcount closes, which other Amazon science org would you want, and why? [Likely] [54]
  8. What science problem in our org do you think is unsolved, and how would you start on it? [Likely]
    • How would you write the 6-page document proposing it?

B. Search (Stores Foundational AI / Search Science, formerly M5)

  1. You have a large collection of user click data for many search queries and product documents. Is click data alone sufficient? How would you train a retrieval or IR model with the data at hand? [Reported] [13]
    • What position, presentation and trust biases contaminate the clicks?
    • How would you estimate relevance from click logs while accounting for query intent? [Likely] (Amazon Science work on intent-based relevance from click logs [29])
  2. Walk through how you would design, train, validate and deploy a ranking model for product search. [Reported] [56]
    • Pointwise, pairwise or listwise learning-to-rank: which, and why?
  3. Walk through a ranking project you shipped: the customer pain point, why that model, which metrics you used, and how you diagnosed a disappointing A/B result. [Reported] (Amazon MLE loop project deep dive, aggregator) [20]
  4. Compare a small and a large model from the same text-embedding family. What do you gain and what do you pay, for first-stage product retrieval? [Reported] (RS breadth round, aggregator) [19]
  5. Compare approaches to building joint image-text embeddings, for example for visual search or for matching product photos to queries. [Reported] (RS online assessment, aggregator) [19]
    • Implement a numerically stable InfoNCE loss that is symmetric across both directions (image to text and text to image) for a batch of paired embeddings. [Reported] (science coding round, aggregator) [19][20][59]
    • How does batch size interact with in-batch negatives, and how would you mine hard negatives from the catalog?
  6. Answer a search-relevance application question for Amazon search. [Reported] [3]
  7. Compare bi-encoders and cross-encoders. Where does each sit in a product-search stack? [Reported] [3]
    • How would you distill the cross-encoder into something that meets a retrieval latency budget?
  8. You have 10k product listings in 100 categories, each with a title, description and photo. How would you categorize them? [Reported] [13]
    • How do you handle a long tail of categories with very few examples?
  9. Design an end-to-end retrieval and ranking pipeline with candidate generation, serving, offline metrics versus online guardrails, A/B testing and position bias. [Reported] (Amazon search/recs/ads MLE loop, aggregator) [20][59]
  10. Design a multi-turn search agent that handles long context. [Reported] (RS loop, aggregator) [19]
    • When would you summarize earlier turns, and when would you retrieve them instead?
  11. The ESCI Shopping Queries dataset labels query-product pairs as Exact, Substitute, Complement or Irrelevant. Why is a 4-way label more useful than binary relevance for Amazon search? [Likely] [34]
    • How would you use Substitute and Complement labels differently in ranking and in recommendations?
    • The dataset covers English, Japanese and Spanish. How would you transfer a relevance model to a new marketplace?
  12. How would you rank with several objectives at once (relevance, conversion, margin, delivery speed)? [Likely]
    • Would you use multi-task learning-to-rank, and how would you weight the tasks (for example uncertainty weighting, PCGrad or Nash-MTL)? [Likely] (Amazon's DeepMTL2R library lists 21 multi-task weighting methods plus a single-task baseline [45])
  13. (Search/IR postings list learning-to-rank and recommender work [26].) How would you select a set of results that is both relevant and diverse from millions of candidates, faster than MMR? [Likely] (MUSS, UAI 2026 [39])
    • When does diversity hurt a high-intent query like a model number?
  14. How would you use an LLM as a relevance judge for product search, and how would you validate it against human annotators? [Likely]
  15. How would you detect and fix wrong attribute values (size, color, material) in catalog listings that hurt search? [Likely]
    • SynthAVE labels (attribute, value) pairs as Correct, Incorrect or Unknown, using majority vote over 21 LLM-judge configurations (7 model families with 3 prompts each). When would you trust an LLM arena as a labeler? [Likely] (EMNLP 2026 Industry [38])
    • In SynthAVE about 39% of pairs are Unknown (the text cannot decide). How would you treat "not determinable" in training and in your metric? [Likely] [38]
  16. Design a system that detects duplicate or variant listings of the same product from different sellers (title, attributes and images), so search does not show the same item five times. [Likely]
    • How do you set the merge threshold when a false merge is costlier than a missed one?

C. Advertising (Sponsored Products, DSP, Ads Trust, Ads measurement)

  1. Explain how transformers and attention work. How does a decoder-only LLM generate text at inference time? (An Ads candidate reported being rejected after not preparing this.) [Reported] [5]
  2. Design a system that recommends trustworthy ads to Amazon users while maintaining brand safety and compliance. [Reported] (Advertising Trust) [8]
    • Walk through the full data pipeline from collection to deployment. [Reported] [8]
    • How do you get labels for rare policy violations?
  3. Science Depth on an Ads loop: your resume, plus GenAI fundamentals, plus the business impact of your work. [Reported] [8]
  4. Amazon Ads generates image creatives for advertisers. Design an evaluation framework that checks whether a generated image spells the brand name correctly and places it naturally. [Reported] (RS and MLE science-application / ML design round, aggregator) [19][20][59]
    • How would you combine OCR, a learned "naturalness" judge and human review, and how would you calibrate the judge?
  5. Design a click counter over a sliding k-minute window, then an advertiser-metrics service with campaign and event entities, windowed pre-aggregation and idempotency for duplicate events. [Reported] (Ads MLE loop, aggregator) [20]
  6. Design click-through-rate or conversion-rate prediction for Sponsored Products. [Likely]
    • Why does calibration matter for auction pricing?
    • How do you handle delayed conversions?
  7. How would you learn a bidding policy offline from logged auction data? [Likely] (AuctionGym, KDD 2023 ADS track [37])
    • Why is offline evaluation of learning-to-bid hard (unobserved confounding, costly experiments)?
    • How does optimal bidding differ between second-price and first-price auctions, and what is bid shading?
  8. How would you measure the incremental lift of an ad campaign for an advertiser? [Likely]
    • Ghost ads, geo experiments or observational causal methods: what are the trade-offs?
  9. How would you blend sponsored products into a generative shopping answer without eroding trust, and how would you measure the effect? [Likely]
  10. Design a budget-pacing algorithm for advertisers. How would you frame it as constrained optimization or as a bandit? [Likely]

D. Alexa AI, AGI and Amazon Nova (foundation models)

  1. Expect several "modern LLM training technique" questions, with follow-ups on each stage (pretraining, SFT, preference tuning). [Reported] (AGI-team role) [6][7] (see chapter 07)
  2. You are handed a Transformer implementation that trains but performs badly. Debug it. [Reported] (Amazon AGI, aggregator) [21]
    • Which bugs in masking, scaling, positional encoding or normalization produce silent quality loss?
  3. Build and train a small classifier live. [Reported] (Amazon AGI, aggregator) [21]
  4. Reinforcement-learning fundamentals plus coding. [Reported] (Amazon AGI, aggregator) [21]
    • Why does GRPO remove the critic, what does that cost, and how would you design the reward? [Reported] (RS phone screen, aggregator) [19]
    • How would you parallelize GRPO training? [Reported] (MLE loop, aggregator) [20]
    • Explain PPO's clipped objective. [Likely] [19]
  5. Write the rotary position embedding (RoPE) formula and explain how it keeps relative position inside attention. [Reported] (MLE loop, aggregator) [20][59]
    • Compare RoPE with ALiBi, and explain KV caching and pipeline parallelism. [Likely] (listed as round topics, not as asked questions, in the RS aggregator [19])
  6. For a residual MLP of width 4096 in BF16, is batch size 1 or 256 compute-bound, and what does that imply for serving an assistant model? [Reported] (MLE breadth round, aggregator) [20]
  7. Architect an AI chatbot, covering intent detection, dialogue state, generation, fallback behavior, evaluation and continuous learning. [Reported] (RS and MLE ML-design rounds, aggregator) [19][20]
    • Where does a classic NLU intent model still beat an LLM for an Alexa-style assistant?
  8. (Stretch, reported from a staff-level loop, so above the L5 bar.) Design a distributed pipeline that ingests, tokenizes, deduplicates and filters pretraining data for a foundation model. [Reported] (aggregator) [20]
    • How would you measure the effect of a quality filter without a full pretraining run?
  9. Discuss LLM decoding and training choices in the context of an Amazon use case. [Reported] (AS II, LLM team, Santa Clara) [12]
    • How does beam search work, and when does it hurt open-ended generation? [Reported] [12]
  10. How would you improve Alexa speech recognition for non-native speakers and users with strong regional accents? [Reported] (Alexa International Tech) [10]
    • Where would you get diverse accent data, and how would you handle phonetic variation? [Reported] [10]
  11. The Alexa International loop is breadth, depth, science application, coding and a bar raiser, with LPs throughout. What would you expect the science-application problem to look like for a multilingual assistant? [Reported for the format] [9]; the follow-up is [Likely]
  12. How would you align a multilingual assistant with SFT, DPO, RLHF or RLAIF when preference data is scarce in some locales? [Likely] (the Alexa International posting lists these methods [23])
  13. Instruction-tuned LLMs are trained on text preferences, but Alexa speaks its answers. What makes a response "speechworthy", and how would you collect preferences for it? [Likely] (Speechworthy Instruction-tuned LMs, EMNLP 2024 [42])
  14. AlexaTM 20B was a multilingual seq2seq model pretrained with denoising plus causal LM. When would an encoder-decoder beat a decoder-only model for an Alexa task? [Likely] [40]
  15. Training LLMs with MXFP4: why can the backward pass run in 4-bit with stochastic rounding and a random Hadamard transform and still give unbiased gradients? [Likely] (AISTATS 2025 [41])
    • What breaks if rounding is biased?
  16. Moving from an ASR, then LLM, then TTS cascade to an end-to-end speech model (in the spirit of Nova Sonic): what changes in latency, evaluation and failure modes? [Likely] [21]
  17. Design a browser-operating agent (in the spirit of Nova Act) that completes multi-step web tasks reliably. [Likely] [21]
    • How do you build its evaluation set and measure step-level versus task-level success?
  18. An Alexa+ style assistant calls real device and service APIs (timers, smart home, ordering). How do you make tool calls reliable and safe? [Likely]
  19. How would you make an on-device wake-word or NLU model 10x smaller with minimal quality loss? [Likely]

E. AWS AI (Bedrock, SageMaker, Q / coding agents, AI research)

  1. Explain transformers, then explain LoRA. [Reported] (AWS AS loop) [4]
    • Where are the LoRA adapters inserted, and what does the rank control?
    • Explain the main parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques, which parameters each trains, and when each beats full fine-tuning. [Reported] (RS and MLE online assessment, aggregator) [19][20]
  2. Explain how an inference engine such as vLLM raises LLM serving throughput (paged KV cache, continuous batching). [Reported] (MLE online assessment, aggregator) [20]
    • Amazon Science's ammo uses multiple agents to optimize vLLM GPU kernels for one deployment. How would you check that an auto-generated kernel is both correct and faster? [Likely] [49]
  3. Design an LLM quality-validation system that gates model or prompt updates before they reach customers. [Reported] (title listed for the RS science-application round, aggregator) [19]
  4. Design an enterprise RAG service. [Reported] [51]
    • How do you enforce document-level access control in retrieval?
  5. Design a GitHub Copilot-style coding agent. [Reported] (Senior AS, Boston) [3]
    • SWE-PolyBench scores agents on repository-level tasks in Java, JavaScript, TypeScript and Python with pass rate and file- and node-retrieval metrics. Why measure retrieval separately from pass rate? [Likely] (arXiv 2025 [46])
  6. Evaluate an LLM agent with metrics beyond accuracy: tool use, hallucination and calibration. [Reported] [3]
  7. RAGChecker separates retriever metrics (claim recall, context precision) from generator metrics (faithfulness, noise sensitivity, hallucination, context utilization). How would you use that split to debug a customer's RAG system? [Likely] (NeurIPS 2024 Datasets and Benchmarks [35])
  8. RefChecker checks LLM outputs at the level of (subject, predicate, object) claim triplets in zero, noisy and accurate context settings. Why triplets rather than sentences? [Likely] (2024 [36])
    • Where does triplet extraction itself fail?
  9. A customer's Bedrock-hosted workload costs too much. How would you cut inference cost sharply without unacceptable quality loss? [Likely] [21] (see chapter 07)
  10. Chronos turns a time series into tokens by scaling and quantization, then trains a language model on them. Why does that work, and what does Chronos-Bolt change (patches, direct multi-step quantile output)? [Likely] (TMLR 2024; Chronos-Bolt Nov 2024 [33])
    • Chronos-2 (Oct 2025) adds multivariate and covariate-informed zero-shot forecasting. How would you evaluate it fairly against a tuned, dataset-specific model?
  11. Design an agent that migrates a whole Java 8 repository to Java 17. How do you decide that the migration succeeded? [Likely] (MigrationBench, KDD 2026 [60])
    • MigrationBench approximates functional equivalence: the build passes, compiled class versions match the target, test methods are unchanged and the test count does not fall. What can still go wrong under these checks?
  12. Coding agents run inside customer workspaces. How would you red-team one for jailbreaks that hide malicious intent in a half-finished multi-file project rather than in the prompt? [Likely] (JAWS-Bench, arXiv 2510.01359 [61])
  13. A customer asks a Bedrock-hosted assistant about AWS services whose behavior changed after the model's training cutoff. How would you detect and handle stale knowledge? [Likely] (evolveQA, EMNLP 2026 [62])
  14. Design a hallucination detector for RAG answers over very long contexts (around 90K characters), with sentence-level labels (Supported, Contradicted, Not Mentioned, Supplementary). [Likely] (TRIVIA+, ACL 2026 [63])
    • Why would you inject controlled label noise into the benchmark?
  15. Synthesize realistic tabular data, or detect anomalies in tabular data, with generative models. What are the evaluation pitfalls? [Likely] (amazon-science TabSyn and AnoLLM repositories [49])

F. Rufus (generative shopping assistant)

  1. Design Rufus: answer "What do I need for cold weather golf?" using the catalog, reviews and community Q&A. [Likely] [22][28]
    • How do you ground answers in product facts and avoid hallucinated specs?
    • How do you evaluate offline before an A/B test, and what is the online success metric?
  2. Design an agentic recommendation system for Amazon, covering components, model choices, and quality and safety evaluation. [Reported] [16]
  3. Rufus runs on several LLMs, including Amazon Nova, on Bedrock. How would you route each query between a small and a large model? [Likely] [27]
  4. How would you personalize answers with a customer's purchase and browse history while respecting privacy? [Likely] [27]
  5. You have one latency budget for an assistant answer (say, first token in under one second). How do you split it across retrieval, reranking, generation and safety filters? [Likely] [22]
  6. How would you summarize thousands of reviews into a short "Customers say" blurb, and measure faithfulness and coverage of minority opinions? [Likely]
  7. Personalized autocomplete for a chat interface: how would you rank candidate completions, and would you report saved-keystrokes@k or acceptance-rate@k? [Likely] (amazon-science personalized chat autocomplete experiments [49])
  8. How would you red-team a shopping assistant for unsafe, biased or policy-violating answers? [Likely]

G. Personalization and recommendations (homepage, Amazon Music, explanations)

  1. Design a pipeline to improve personalized product recommendations on the Amazon homepage. [Reported] [16]
    • What is the north-star metric, and what are the guardrails?
  2. A user goes from viewing to clicking to purchasing. Model the probability of each action given a user and a product. [Reported] [14]
    • Three independent models, or a joint model? [Reported] [14]
    • How would you build the dataset and down-sample (for example with reservoir sampling)? [Reported] [14]
  3. Design a recommender system end to end. [Reported] (reported as the design round in an AS loop) [57]
    • How do you handle cold start for new users and new ASINs?
  4. You have logged rankings from the Amazon Music home page with logging-policy propensities. How would you estimate a new ranking policy's value offline? [Likely] (Music off-policy evaluation benchmark [44])
    • Compare IPS, self-normalized IPS and doubly-robust estimators for slate rankings.
    • How do you check whether your OPE estimate matches the online A/B result?
  5. You have a pool of LLM-generated explanations for a recommendation. Would you choose among them with RL fine-tuning (PPO, GRPO, DPO) or with a pairwise ranker? [Likely] (RecSys 2026 finding that LambdaRank beat single-action RL [47])
  6. How would you avoid feedback loops that recommend only already-popular items? [Likely]
  7. How would you personalize email or push notifications as uplift modeling rather than response modeling? [Likely]

H. SCOT (Supply Chain Optimization Technologies), forecasting and inventory

  1. What is the newsvendor problem, and how would you use it to set inventory levels? [Reported] (Amazon supply-chain interviews) [15]
    • How does a quantile forecast map to the critical ratio?
  2. Model an optimization problem from your own work: objective, decision variables and constraints. [Reported] [52]
    • When would you use a heuristic instead of an exact solver?
  3. How would you forecast demand for millions of products, including new ones with no history? [Likely] [30]
    • Why do buying decisions need probabilistic or quantile forecasts rather than point forecasts?
    • Would you use a global model or per-item models?
  4. Design a system that decides how much of each product to buy, from which vendor, and when. [Likely] (SCOT Optimal Sourcing / Buying Science posting [24])
  5. Design inventory placement across fulfillment centers. How do forecast errors propagate into transportation cost? [Likely] [31]
  6. How do you forecast through Prime Day when history is sparse and demand was censored by stock-outs? [Likely]
  7. Could a pretrained foundation model like Chronos replace your team's per-domain forecaster? What evidence would convince you? [Likely] [33]
  8. Design a delivery-promise prediction (the date shown on the detail page). What loss would you use if late costs more than early? [Likely] (Last Mile candidate context [11])
  9. How would you run an experiment on a buying policy when products share warehouses and trucks (interference)? [Likely]

I. Pricing and causal inference

  1. Design a detector that catches pricing errors (for example a $500 item listed at $5) before they go live, across millions of ASINs and sellers. [Likely]
    • How do you trade off blocking a real deal against letting a costly error through?
  2. Design a system that recommends competitive prices for millions of products. [Likely]
    • How do you estimate price elasticity when your own algorithm set the prices (endogeneity)?
    • What guardrails prevent price spirals and loss of customer trust?
  3. How would you measure the effect of a pricing algorithm when prices interact across products? [Likely]
    • Switchback, cluster-randomized or synthetic-control designs: when would you use each?
  4. How would you validate a causal model before trusting it? [Likely] (Amazon's causal-validation package simulates data with a known effect and supports placebo tests [48])
    • Design a placebo test for a synthetic-control estimate of a price change.
  5. How would you design an A/B test for a model launch, and what would you do if it is underpowered? [Reported] [58]
  6. How would you decide whether to discount a product for a deal event, accounting for cannibalization and pull-forward demand? [Likely]
  7. When would you use double ML, instrumental variables or difference-in-differences for a promotion's effect? [Likely]

J. Prime Video (and other Amazon content and devices teams)

No first-person Prime Video AS reports were found in this run. Everything below is [Likely].

  1. Design recommendations for Prime Video, where viewing is sequential and feedback is implicit. [Likely] - Is a show watched for 3 minutes a positive or a negative label?
  2. How would you choose artwork or thumbnails per user, and evaluate the policy offline? [Likely]
  3. How would you detect video-quality problems (buffering, artifacts, audio-video sync) at scale from playback telemetry? [Likely]
  4. Design ad insertion for ad-supported streaming: where to place breaks, and how to measure viewer drop-off. [Likely]
  5. How would you forecast live-sports streaming demand for capacity planning? [Likely]
  6. How would you detect and skip intros and recaps, or generate scene-level metadata, with video models? [Likely]

K. Payments, trust, fraud and abuse

  1. Design a model to predict payment-method failure and pick the best fallback for the customer. [Likely] (Amazon Payment Products L5 posting [25])
  2. Train and evaluate a fraud model under extreme class imbalance, delayed chargeback labels and adaptive adversaries. [Likely] - Amazon's Fraud Dataset Benchmark groups public datasets by fraud type (card-not-present, bots, malicious traffic, content moderation, credit risk). How would you check that a method generalizes across them? [Likely] [43]
  3. Report precision, recall and AUC for imbalanced fraud data, and say which you would optimize. [Likely] (the RS aggregator lists imbalanced metrics as a round topic, not as an asked question [19])
  4. Use computer vision to detect defective products in fulfillment-center warehouses. [Reported] [18]

L. "Have you read our work?" Recent Amazon Science publications as interview questions

These are [Likely] prompts. HMs often ask "What do you think of X?" about their own team's work. Read the abstract and the evaluation section of anything in your target org.

  1. Chronos (TMLR 2024), Chronos-Bolt (Nov 2024) and Chronos-2 (arXiv, Oct 2025; released to SageMaker JumpStart in Dec 2025 and AutoGluon-Cloud in Jun 2026): what is the weakest part of the "language of time series" argument? [Likely] [33]
  2. Shopping Queries Dataset (ESCI, arXiv 2022, used in a KDD Cup): what does ESCI miss that real Amazon search needs (personalization, freshness, session context)? [Likely] [34]
  3. RAGChecker (NeurIPS 2024 D&B) and RefChecker (2024): how would you meta-evaluate an automatic hallucination checker against human judgment? [Likely] [35][36]
  4. AuctionGym (KDD 2023 ADS; AdKDD 2022 best paper): what does a simulator buy you that logged data cannot, and what does it cost you? [Likely] [37]
  5. SynthAVE (EMNLP 2026 Industry): what are the risks of evaluating on synthetically generated products with anonymized brands? [Likely] [38]
  6. MUSS (UAI 2026): MMR is a special case of MUSS with one partition. What does the constant-factor approximation guarantee buy you in practice? [Likely] [39]
  7. Training LLMs with MXFP4 (AISTATS 2025): why target the backward pass first? [Likely] [41]
  8. Speechworthy instruction-tuned LMs (EMNLP 2024): how would you extend a listening-based preference dataset to multi-turn voice? [Likely] [42]
  9. Multimodal chain-of-thought (mm-cot) and Auto-CoT: are these still relevant once reasoning models are trained with RL? [Likely] [49]
  10. Pairwise ranking beats single-action RL for offline explanation selection (RecSys 2026): when would you expect that result to flip? [Likely] [47]
  11. Amazon Science's 2026 open-source releases include agent self-improvement recipes (Ratchet, Double Ratchet), RL-training kernels (DualKV shared-prompt FlashAttention), multi-agent vLLM kernel optimization (ammo) and long-context hallucination benchmarks (ACL 2026). Pick one relevant to this team and critique it. [Likely] [49]
  12. Select, Label, Evaluate: Active Testing in NLP (EMNLP 2026) reports performance estimates within 1% of the full test set using up to 95% fewer labels. With a fixed annotation budget, how would you choose which examples to label to estimate a new model's accuracy? [Likely] [64]
  13. What Fits (Into Few Tokens) Doesn't Overfit (arXiv 2026) argues that strategies which compress into a short description overfit a reused validation set less. Your team has tuned against the same offline benchmark for two years. How would you check whether it is overfit? [Likely] [65]
  14. ReSkill (arXiv 2026) treats creating reusable skills as part of the agentic RL loop rather than a separate step. What could go wrong when skills and the policy evolve together, and how would you evaluate it? [Likely] [66]
  15. Adaptively looped diffusion language models (ALoDLM, arXiv 2610.04198) give hard tokens more refinement passes than easy ones. Would you consider a diffusion LM for a latency-bound assistant, and what would you measure first? [Likely] [67]
  16. WebIE (ACL 2023) targets faithful information extraction from web text. How would you extract product attributes from noisy seller descriptions without inventing facts that are not in the text? [Likely] [68]
  17. CaML (WWW 2023) maps retail products to emission-factor categories with zero-shot text similarity. How would you evaluate a zero-shot product-to-taxonomy matcher when you have only a few hundred human labels? [Likely] [69]
  18. Earlier LLM distillation work, "Learning with less: knowledge distillation from LLMs via unlabeled data": when is distilling an LLM into a small classifier the right call for a production team? [Likely] [32]

M. L5-bar probes that recur across all teams

  1. Your model's online A/B result fell short of its offline gain. How do you find out why? [Reported] (RS depth round, aggregator) [19] - Separate the causes: offline metric mismatch, train-serve skew, position or presentation effects, novelty, and an underpowered test.
  2. Of everything you proposed for our problem, what is the riskiest assumption, and how would you de-risk it in two weeks? [Likely]
  3. Your design improves our team metric but hurts a sister team's metric (for example, conversion up and returns up). How do you decide? [Likely]
  4. Which parts would you build, and which would you reuse from existing Amazon services or other science teams? [Likely]
  5. How would you hand this system to engineering for long-term ownership, with monitoring and retraining triggers? [Likely]
  6. Tell me about a time you influenced a team outside your own with your science. How would that look here? [Likely] (L5 scope: influence on peers and sister teams; see chapter 01)
